DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
Executive summary
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
The case in brief
The Western Cape can grow international arrivals from about 1.5 million in 2025 to 2.2
million by 2035 on the base path, and to 2.5 million on the accelerated path. Gateway
capacity sets the pace in the summer peak; demand and conversion set it in the green
season and beyond the Metro.
From 1,498,829 arrivals in 2025 the growth model reaches 2,175,778 on the base path and 2,516,282 on
the accelerated path in 2035.1
International passenger arrivals at Cape Town International, the series
ACSA reports, stood at 1.44m in FY24/25 and 1.57m in FY25/26.2
The study tested an eighteen-entry hypothesis tree against seven deliverables and four working analyses
between December 2025 and October 2026. This report sets out what the evidence found, what Wesgro
and its partners should do and how progress will be measured.
Five constraints cap the case
• The gateway. The growth model draws current air access as a line at 1.8 million inbound seats,
and the base case crosses it around 2030. Runway relief is planned for about 2033, though ACSA
now schedules the realigned runway for December 2029.3
• Full routes. Eleven of 27 tabled international routes run above 80% load, and carriers on full routes
raise fares before they add seats.4
• Seasons. June holds 6.4% of the year's international seats, and about 62% of overseas visitor
travel falls between October and March.5
• Reach. Accessibility falls from 4.7 out of 5 in the Cape Metro to 1.0 in the Central Karoo, and one
touring route of six is rated Ready.6
• Levers held elsewhere. Visas, air service agreements and the airport and port programmes sit
with national government and SOEs, and no approved method exists for provincial tourism GDP
and jobs.7

Strategy on a page
The updated hypothesis tree holds three pillars, six themes and twenty focus areas. Ten focus areas
carried through as Validated and six were reworded to the lever the evidence supports. Of the other four,
one was refined, one is new, one moved between pillars and one is flagged for analysis still to be
commissioned.
Figure ES.1. The updated hypothesis tree
Source: Current State Diagnostic, Dec 2025, p64 (December tree); dispositions as recorded at the 14 October 2026 review; traceability table,
Appendix A1.
How the tree changed since December
Eight December entries changed scope, place or form, and one focus area was added. The changes point
the same way: from general ambitions to the specific lever the evidence supports, and from provincial
supply to national escalation where the lever sits nationally.

Twenty focus areas
Each focus area below carries the one-sentence finding that opens its Chapter 3 entry, unchanged. The
entry itself gives the December hypothesis, the evidence with page references, the recommendation split
between Wesgro, its partners and the national tier, a benchmark precedent and the initiative and
indicators it leads to.
Table ES.2. Pillar 1, Reshape demand: conversion, not awareness, holds back the growth markets, the green season
and the province beyond its core.
Focus area Disposition What the evidence shows and what follows Initiative
1 Respond to growth
segment traveller
preferences
Validated Four traveller segments, each concentrated in named home
markets, give Wesgro a sharper target than country alone,
and the province scores 3.7 to 4.6 out of 5 on its ability to win
them.
I1
2 Create green-season
demand with MICE and
events
Reworded The green season is a seat problem and an events problem at
once. June holds 6.4% of the year's international seats, and
business events are the lever best placed to fill the trough,
though international meetings now peak from September to
November.
I2
3 Make the whole
province findable and
bookable
Reworded Outside the Cape Peninsula, the Winelands and the Garden
Route, most product cannot be booked where travellers book,
so interest the province has already earned has nowhere to
land.
I3
4 Convert India and
China hub traffic
Reworded About 101,000 Indian and Chinese travellers a year already
reach Cape Town, mainly through Gulf and African hubs, and
with no direct Indian route likely before 2030, converting that
flow is the lever available now.
I4
5 Increase yield in
mature markets
Validated The mature markets already pay the most and fill the fullest
routes, so growth there comes from more value per visitor and
more seats on existing routes, not from more marketing.

foundation (Africa)
Validated Regional Africa is the province's steadiest volume and its least
developed premium market: Zimbabwe and Namibia supply
62% of African arrivals, while West African visitors spend the
most per visit.

The growth case and its sequence
The three paths differ by intervention, not by demand potential.8
The base path needs hub carriers to keep
adding capacity and Wesgro's programmes to expand. The accelerated path also needs direct India and
China routes and dedicated activation in India, China, the USA and the Gulf; without confirmed routes,
volume reverts to base.9
On the eleven full routes, marketing cannot make up for missing seats, because
carriers raise fares instead.10
On the hub routes, which run at 65% to 72% load and add the most base-
case arrivals, it can.11
Figure ES.2. Arrivals paths to 2035
Source: Market Assessment Report, May 2026, p19 (2025 base); Demand-Side Analysis, Jun 2026, p20, p21. The 2031 base value and the
accelerated and conservative milestones are computed (section 5.3).
Air access and bookability move first, because every other lever runs through them. Phase 1, Activate
(2027 to 2028), holds and adds seats on the full routes, builds the green-season calendar and counts and
closes the bookability gap. It also starts two or three inclusion pilots and sets up Steerco, the data protocol
and the national escalation agenda. Phase 2, Expand (2029 to 2031), scales what works and meets the
model's seat line at the 2030 mid-term review. Phase 3, Sustain (2032 to 2035), takes the direct route
cases to carriers as runway capacity arrives.
Eighteen initiatives carry the plan, one per focus area except where one owner delivers two. Wesgro is
accountable for twelve; DEDAT, the Department of Mobility, municipalities and site operators lead the
other six. Thirteen draw mainly on Wesgro's operating budget. None has yet been costed, and setting the
rand bands is the first funding task (section 4.6).

DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
Chapter 1
Context
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
1.1 Mandate and objective
Wesgro commissioned a strategy to "double international tourist arrivals to the Western
Cape by 2035"; the evidence has reframed that ambition as growth to 2.5 million arrivals,
paced by gateway capacity in the summer peak and by demand in the green season.
Wesgro's mandate
Wesgro's mandate frames this strategy. The agency promotes tourism, trade and investment for Cape
Town and the Western Cape, and the tourism goal sits inside that wider economic brief. Its mission is to
work "together with partners, drive inclusive and sustainable economic growth, strengthen
competitiveness, and create jobs in Cape Town and the Western Cape".12
Its vision is to "be a leading regional tourism, trade and investment promotion agency".13
Two points in the
mission matter for what follows: growth is a means to jobs, and Wesgro acts through partners rather than
holding the levers itself.
Wesgro's strategy runs to eight statements, quoted here as printed:14
• "Ensure that Cape Town and the Western Cape is top of mind as a leading business and tourism
destination with premium products and services."
• "Prioritise new investment into identified export priority areas"
• "Increase re-investment and expansion from existing WC businesses"
• "Unlock access to new markets and new buyers - China, ASEAN, India, Brazil & Middle East, while
maintaining a continued focus on established markets"
• "Enhance and replicate catalytic delivery models to deliver economic impact with partners in key
areas"
• "Track and address systemic challenges influencing economic competitiveness."
• "Provide economic data, insights & analytics for the Western Cape."
• "Digitally transform across all our mandates to place clients at the centre of how we deliver services,
enable staff and to empower stakeholders."
Four of the eight bear directly on this study. The first sets the destination proposition and the fourth names
the new buyer markets while holding the established ones. The sixth commits Wesgro to act on systemic
constraints to competitiveness and the seventh makes it the province's source of economic data.
The study objective and the original ambitions
The study objective was set in November 2025. It reads in full: "To develop an evidence-based tourism
strategy that will double international tourist arrivals to the Western Cape by 2035, through a holistic
approach that drives demand, strengthens supply and enables inclusive, sustainable and geographically
dispersed growth aligned with WESGRO's strategic priorities".15
That sentence fixes the three pillars the
report keeps throughout: demand, supply and enablement.
Four headline ambitions sat under the objective: 2.8 million international visitors, 300,000 tourism-linked
jobs, 10,000 SMMEs onboarded and twice the airlift and cruise volumes.16
None carried a base year, a
baseline value or a definition; the only year on the slide is 2035. A month later the Current State
12
Inception Report, Nov 2025, p5.
13
Inception Report, Nov 2025, p5.

Inception Report, Nov 2025, p5.

Inception Report, Nov 2025, p4.
Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory 24
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
Diagnostic restated the ambition as 5.7 million arrivals and R54.7 billion in tourism spend by 2035, again
without a base.17
The two statements do not reconcile with each other or with the measured baseline. Cape Town
International recorded 1.44 million international passenger arrivals in FY24/25.18
Twice that is 2.88 million,
close to the 2.8 million ambition and nowhere near 5.7 million, and neither ambition names the series it
counts.
The demand model tested the ambition and reframed it. From a 2025 base of 1,498,829 international
arrivals, the base case reaches 2,175,778 by 2035 and the accelerated case 2,516,282, with a
conservative floor of 1,822,634.19
The accelerated case is what this strategy calls growth to 2.5 million,
and it is the goal the report carries.
The reframing rests on what the model says about access: the base case assumes Wesgro's effort at
2025 intensity, selective frequency gains on existing routes and continued hub expansion.20
The
accelerated case adds confirmed direct routes to India and China, above-baseline activation and more
hub capacity into Cape Town.21
The model's own footnote is blunt: without confirmed routes, volume
reverts to base.22
Even the base case runs into the model's seat line. The growth model draws inbound capacity at 1.8
million seats, and the base case first passes that line at the 2030 plotted year, at 1,802,929 arrivals.23
Growth to 2.5 million is therefore a supply programme before it is a marketing programme, and every
chapter of this report returns to that point.
The reframing was tabled with Wesgro at the summary-of-findings session of 14 October 2026 [Summary
of Findings, 14 Oct 2026, page to be confirmed against the final deck]. The table below sets each original
ambition against the baseline in hand and the form in which the strategy now carries it.

Wesgro's outcome metrics
Wesgro measures itself against six strategic outcomes, each with one metric.24
The slide prints no period
for any of them [Wesgro to confirm the period over which the six outcome metrics are measured]. The six
are:
• A differentiated Cape Town and the Western Cape as a leading regional economy: 120 million
audience reached to consider Cape Town and the Western Cape as a leading trade and tourism
destination
• Increased foreign and domestic investment: R30 billion new investment with a focus on targeted
areas and markets
• Growth in Western Cape exports of goods and services: R9.1 billion new export declarations for
goods and services
• Growth in leisure tourism consideration in international and local markets: 800,000 to 1 million new
international tourist arrivals
• Growth in business events hosted in the Western Cape: 161 business event bids secured with an
estimated economic value of R4.5 billion
• A future-fit and trusted Wesgro: clean audit, satisfied clients and low culture entropy
Two of the six are tourism metrics, and the period decides how they compare with the growth case. The
base case adds 676,949 arrivals between 2025 and 2035 and the accelerated case 1,017,453,25
so
800,000 to 1 million new arrivals sits between the two paths over a ten-year horizon. Over a horizon to
2030 it sits well above the base case, which adds about 304,000 by then (1,802,929 against 1,498,829).26
The business-events metric ties to the green-season case. International association meetings in Cape
Town rose from 33 in 2022 to 58 in 2024 and are "primarily hosted between May and November".27
That
window takes in five of the six green-season months, April to September.28
Chapter 5 fixes the period for
both metrics and sets them against the growth case on one basis.
Alignment with provincial strategy
The SLA requires this report to align with the Growth for Jobs Strategy and with Wesgro's five-year plan.29
The inception report carries the same instruction: recommendations are to align with both the G4J strategy
and Wesgro's FY26 to FY30 plan.30
The report is the Final Research Report under clause 1.1.14. Chapter
5 answers the monitoring and evaluation clauses 1.1.4 and 1.1.10 [clause references to be checked
against the signed SLA Annexure B, whose wording is not in hand].
The Growth for Jobs Strategy 2023, approved by Cabinet, was among the first documents Wesgro
supplied to the study.31
It designates tourism a primary tradable sector for the province.32
Its arrivals target
is 3 million international tourist arrivals by 2035 on the South African Tourism basis, built as 2 million in
2019 plus 1 million.33
If the model counts arrivals on the SA Tourism basis, G4J's 3 million for 2035 sits
above both paths. The difference is method: G4J adds 1 million to 2019's 2 million, while the growth model
builds up market by market against the seats and routes the gateway can carry. [G4J 2023 document to
be read and the 3 million target confirmed against it.]

The G4J figure and the study's figures count different things. Three million is provincial tourist arrivals as
South African Tourism measures them; 1.44 million is passenger arrivals through one airport as ACSA
counts them. On the SAT basis the province received 1,538,142 international tourist arrivals in 2025, an
11.1% increase.34
The strategy's 2.5 million therefore cannot be set against the 3 million without
conversion.
Wesgro's FY26 to FY30 plan is the second alignment test. A Wesgro five-year strategy was supplied to
the study,35
but no plan document is in hand. Alignment is therefore stated at the level the inception report
gives it [Wesgro to supply the FY26 to FY30 plan and its tourism targets, so that the Chapter 5 baselines
and the Chapter 4 phase gates can be mapped to the plan cycle]. Chapter 4 sets its first decision gate at
the end of 2028, within the plan period and before the base case crosses the model's seat line.

Three rules follow. Airport passenger arrivals at Cape Town International, by fiscal year, are the baseline
and tracking series, with FY24/25 at 1.44 million anchoring 1.2 and the Chapter 5 baseline year. The
demand model's 2025 base and its 2035 points are the target series, and South African Tourism's
provincial count is used once, to read across to the G4J target.
One caution closes the section: the FY25/26 actual of 1.57 million already sits above the model's 2025
base and below its 2028 base-case point of 1,673,855.38
The two series differ in measure and period, so
the gap is not evidence that the model is cautious. The report quotes them side by side and never adds
one to the other [model owner to confirm the series and year basis behind the 2025 base of 1,498,829].
1.2 The Western Cape tourism system today
The Western Cape's international tourism has recovered to a record, and the system that
delivered the recovery runs through five source markets, six summer months and one metro.
Arrivals and recovery
International passenger arrivals at Cape Town International reached 1.44m in FY24/25, against 1.16m in
FY19/20, the last pre-pandemic year.39
Arrivals passed the pre-pandemic peak of 1.19m, set in FY18/19,
during FY23/24, when they reached 1.36m.40
FY25/26 added a further step to 1.57m.41
The fall and the climb were both steep. Arrivals dropped to 80,400 in FY20/21 and 378,500 in FY21/22,
then returned to 1.07m in FY22/23.42
Two-way international passengers reached 2.90m in FY24/25, 29%
above FY17/18,43
and 3.3m in calendar 2025 inside a total of 11.1m, both up 7% on the year.44
Cape Town's recovery also ran ahead of the national one, so the destination gained share as it recovered.
In the first half of 2024 its air arrivals had recovered further against 2019 than South Africa's in eight of the
ten largest markets.45
The USA stood at 162% and Australia at 172% of their 2019 level.46

Three arrivals measures
The 1.44m is ACSA's count of international passengers arriving at Cape Town International in the
fiscal year. It is the series this report uses as its recovery baseline and its monitoring base year.47
South African Tourism counts tourist arrivals to the province, which the Demand-Side Analysis notes
are distinct from airport passenger traffic.48
The growth model in Chapter 2 runs from a third series, a
2025 base of 1,498,829 international arrivals.49
The deliverables do not reconcile the three, so the report names the measure each time it cites an
arrivals figure.
Figure 1.1. Airport arrivals, FY15/16 to FY25/26
Source: Current State Diagnostic, Dec 2025, p25 (FY15/16 to FY24/25); Demand-Side Analysis, Jun 2026, p11 (FY25/26). CTIA and ACSA

Spend and value
Foreign direct spend in the province rose from R22.7bn in FY2024 to R25.9bn in FY2025, a gain of 14% in
one year.50
The Western Cape earns that spend from a modest share of the national market. Across the
four quarters of 2025 it took 16%, 13%, 13% and 16% of South Africa's foreign arrivals and 26%, 20%,
22% and 27% of foreign direct spend.51
The gap between the two shares is the province's premium. About a quarter of national spend from about
a seventh of arrivals means the average foreign visitor to the Western Cape spends roughly 1.6 times the
national average.52
The province earns about US$890 per international arrival, against US$500 for the rest
of South Africa and US$630 for Morocco, though below Kenya's US$1,460.53

More than a quarter of overseas visitors stay between 7 and 21 days, on printed bands that sum to only
48%.54
Some 5% stay 1 to 2 days, 12% 3 to 4, 14% 7 to 10, 3% 11 to 14, 13% 15 to 21 and 1% other.55
The Diagnostic prints the same distribution from the 2024 Wesgro Visitor Tracking Survey, in which
52.33% of respondents gave no length of stay, so the bands describe about half the sample.56
The modal visitor in the 2024 Wesgro survey spends R1,000 to R2,000 a day, is aged 36 to 50 and travels
as a pair.57
Nearly 40% spend more than R1,000 a day.58
Self-catering at 17.4% and guesthouses at 9.7%
lead accommodation choice, ahead of hotels at 3.6%;59
together these shares cover under a third of
respondents.
Source markets
Five markets supply 63% of overseas arrivals. In 2025 the UK held 19%, Germany 17%, the USA 16%,
the Netherlands 7% and France 4%, and the three largest alone held 52%.60
The report uses 63%, the
sum of the printed shares.
Concentration is not new. On the Cape Town overseas air-arrivals basis for January to August 2024 the
same five markets held 61%, with the UK and the USA at 18% each and Germany at 15%.61
The growth
markets remain small: India supplied about 2.6% of South Africa's overseas tourists in September 2025,
the Middle East 2.3% and China 1.4%.62
The mature markets also pay the most. Average spend per visitor in 2024 ran from R32,900 for the USA,
R26,500 for the UK and R25,600 for Germany down to R8,600 for Namibia and R8,500 for Zimbabwe.63
The spread is 3.9 times, and the two regional markets compensate with stability, repeat demand and
counter-seasonal volume.64
Most of these visitors arrive through someone else's hub. The UAE route carried 222,923 air arrivals into
Cape Town in 2025, the UK 158,448, Qatar 126,078, Ethiopia 123,250 and Turkiye 89,090.65
The four hub
routes through Dubai, Doha, Addis Ababa and Istanbul carried 53% of arrivals on the ten largest routes,66
so the hubs, not the source markets, are the province's working gateways.

Air and sea access
Ten airlines fly to Cape Town International from Africa, nine from Europe, three from North and South
America, two from the Middle East and one from Asia.72
The aviation assessment states the gap plainly as
"the absence of any direct capacity to any of the Asian markets", India and China included.73
Twenty-nine
international routes serve the airport in 2026.74
Cape Town International itself is now the physical ceiling. One runway with no parallel taxiway caps
movements at about 30 an hour, and ACSA's programme of about R11.3bn brings relief from about 2032
for the international terminal and about 2033 for the runway.75
ACSA's latest schedule shows the realigned
runway from December 2026 to December 2029, subject to contract award.76
The secondary gateways do
not change the picture: George, above its design limit at 911,000 passengers in 2025, doubles by 2028 or
2029, with construction from October 2026,77
and Cape Winelands Airport targets 2028 with no named
carrier.78
Cruise has grown without new berth capacity. The province hosted 70, 67 and 79 vessels in the 2022/23,
2023/24 and 2024/25 seasons, carrying 144,125, 109,884 and 127,178 passengers.79
The Infrastructure
Report counts 83 calls at the Cape Town berth in 2024/25, 127,000 passengers and R1.79bn of economic
impact.80
[StratEcon 2024/25 cruise report to confirm whether 79 vessels and 83 calls differ by definition.]
Port reliability, not berth space, is the risk. The single berth ran at about 40% of a season capacity of 150
or more calls, and its limits are a 300m ship length and the South-Easter wind.81
The Diagnostic calls the

Port of Cape Town "a binding constraint, with congestion and equipment failures affecting export and

Geography of the system
The Cape Metro generates 72.5% of provincial GDPR, a whole-economy share rather than a tourism
one.83
The Cape Winelands follows at 11.2%, the Garden Route at 7.6%, the West Coast at 4.8%, the
Overberg at 3% and the Central Karoo at 0.5%.84
The decks date only the Garden Route share, to 2023,
while the Metro chart is titled 2024 and the rest carry no year.85
Tourism supply is concentrated in the same way. The Infrastructure Report mapped 50,332 tourism assets
across the six regions, of which the Metro holds 20,281, or 40.3%.86
The Garden Route holds 9,875, the
Cape Winelands 7,730, the Overberg 6,176, the West Coast 4,752 and the Central Karoo 1,518.87
Cape Town is the axle of the system, "the province's global shopfront and primary dispersal hub" in the
Infrastructure Report's words.88
The Diagnostic warns that "any capacity failure in Cape Town quickly
becomes a provincial risk, not just a city problem".89
International visitors land there and disperse from
there, and readiness falls with each step away: accessibility scores drop from 4.7 in the Metro to 1.0 in the
Central Karoo.90
Beyond the Metro the visitor drives. The N2 is "the only continuous inter-regional tourism corridor, creating
a single point of failure", and large parts of the province outside the Metro and the Garden Route remain
effectively car-only destinations.91
The further from Cape Town, the faster the infrastructure falls away.92

Passenger rail has collapsed as a mode. Trainsets fell from about 110 in 1995 to about 91, pushing some
400,000 commuters onto saturated roads, and congestion in the Metro costs an estimated R2.8bn a

year.93
Metrorail still runs 460km, five lines and 104 of 121 stations, with 22.7m passenger trips in FY2025,
but no line reaches the airport and it is rated functionally unusable for tourism.94
Utilities cap the periphery before demand does. Water scarcity in high-growth nodes such as the Garden
Route acts as a "hard ceiling" for visitor load, and load shedding raises costs and degrades the
experience in peripheral districts.95
The airport has no fuel pipeline and depends on road tankers,96
and
the 2017 to 2018 water crisis left a lasting perception of resource pressure.97
The remedies are named but not scheduled. The Diagnostic lists four transport enablers, none dated or
costed: the Cape Town Integrator, rail restoration and devolution, a high-speed city to airport link and
George to Cape Winelands passenger rail.98
PRASA's R21.1bn programme and the City's R123bn, 30-
year devolution case cover fleet and lines, and neither carries a tourism product or an airport link.99
Institutions
Wesgro runs the demand side of this system through six units: Cape Town Air Access, Cruise Cape
Town, the Convention Bureau, Destination Marketing, Research and Tourism.100
Around them sit fifteen
external organisations, from national agencies and state-owned entities to the province, the City,
destination and attraction bodies and private operators.101
The table below sets out who holds what.
The levers that bind the system sit at different tiers. National government holds visa rules, border
compliance, air service agreements and route rights.102
The province holds jobs, inclusion and spatial
outcomes, sector development and infrastructure planning; local government holds service delivery,
precinct management and events readiness; industry holds route viability, demand generation and product
standards.103
Alignment across those tiers is thin. Stakeholders described strain at SA Tourism and a lack of fiscal
space.104
More than twenty bodies produce tourism data and "there is no single view of visitor behaviour,
making strategy reactive rather than predictive".105
Stakeholders say the same. Fifteen of the sixteen organisations interviewed raised industry and
government collaboration.106
The Department of Economic Development named restrictive bilateral air
agreements and destination funding materially below peer destinations as high-impact constraints.107

None of these constraints is a shortage of visitors. The runway, the single corridor, a rail network visitors
cannot use, the thin winter schedule and decision rights split across three tiers all sit outside a marketing
budget. The system has recovered; its constraints are now physical and institutional.
1.3 From recovery to growth
The recovery is complete; gateway capacity caps growth in the summer peak, and demand
and conversion cap it in the green season.
The limits of recovery logic
The study set out in December 2025 to move the destination from recovery to growth; the hypothesis
tree's own framing reads that "the Western Cape must transition from recovery to growth".108
The first half
of that task is done. International passenger arrivals at Cape Town International reached 1.44m in
FY24/25 against 1.16m in FY19/20, and 1.57m in FY25/26.109
Recovery logic assumed capacity waiting to be refilled. Arrivals fell to 80,400 in FY20/21,110
and the task
was to refill idle seats, rooms and routes. That slack is gone. South Africa's international seat capacity is
back at pre-pandemic levels, and Cape Town International runs the highest average load factor of the
country's five largest airports, 79% in 2025.111
Cape Town did more than refill its seats. It added 17 mainline international routes between 2015 and
2025, more than any of eight peer airports, and MIDAS calls the result "a 'what next' moment".112
The
rebound that a recovery plan is built to capture has already happened.
Seats in summer, demand in winter
Growth is now supply-constrained in the summer peak and demand-constrained in the green season,
when airlines withdraw seats: June carries 6.4% of the year's international capacity.113
Skift's survey of 34
outbound markets counts 97 million high-value, South Africa-interested travellers, of whom only 1.4 million

The report's conclusion is that "the challenge is not demand generation but

The binding supply is peak capacity at the gateway. The growth model draws current air access as a line
at 1.8 million inbound seats, and the base case, in which Wesgro maintains current activity, reaches
1,802,929 arrivals at the 2030 plotted year.116

The line is the model's assumption, not a measured cap. At Cape Town International's 79% average load
factor, 1.8 million inbound seats would carry about 1.42 million arrivals, fewer than the 1.57 million ACSA
recorded in FY25/26.117
MIDAS tables 3,816,797 international seats for the year to November 2025, more
than 1.8 million each way even if that total counts both directions.118
What binds is the runway at peak
hours in the summer peak, at about 30 movements an hour.119
[Open item: ACSA or OAG to supply peak-
hour and seasonal slot headroom at Cape Town International.]
The routes that exist are full. Eleven of the 27 international routes in the load-factor table ran above 80%
in the year to November 2025, and the network averaged 76.0% on the printed total.120
MIDAS tables 27
routes for the year to November 2025; 29 serve the airport in 2026. Five of the eleven are mature long-
haul routes, led by Newark at 88.6%, Atlanta at 84.3% and Frankfurt and London Heathrow at 83.0%.121
Full routes do not add seats of their own accord. MIDAS expects that "rather than increase capacity
carriers will seek to increase revenues via higher selling fares rather than add new services".122
Airlines

also pull seats out of winter: June carries 6.4% of the year's international capacity and December
10.8%.123
Behind the routes sits the runway. "The airport, not a shortage of visitors is what now caps Cape Town's
tourism growth".124
One runway with no parallel taxiway caps movements at about 30 an hour, and
ACSA's programme of about R11.3bn brings relief from about 2032 for the terminal and about 2033 for the
runway.125
The alternatives do not close the gap in time. George, domestic only, doubles by 2028 or 2029, and Cape
Winelands Airport targets 2028 with no named carrier. In the Infrastructure Report's words, "the certain fix
is the latest, and the earliest big-capacity option is the least certain".126
Route rights sit a tier up: air service
agreements are a national function.127
The Department of Economic Development rates restrictive bilateral
agreements a high-impact constraint on airlift.128
Car-only regions and unbookable product
Beyond the Metro the constraint changes shape, not nature. Accessibility scores fall from 4.7 in the Cape
Metro to 1.0 in the Central Karoo.129
Large parts of the province outside the Metro and the Garden Route
remain effectively car-only destinations.130
The further from Cape Town, the faster the infrastructure falls
away.131
Much of what the car can reach cannot be booked. Most rural properties have no OTA presence and
Route 62 has the lowest online bookability of the six touring routes.132
Even CapeNature takes about 70%
of its reservations through a call centre rather than its online platform.133
Growth above the norm
Global tourism grows 3 to 5% a year under stable conditions, the band the Benchmarking Report calls
organic growth.134
Five of the six comparator destinations grew faster.135
Chile grew 40.4%, Singapore
21%, Kenya 14.6%, Valencia 13 to 14% and New Zealand about 12%, against global growth of 11% in
2024.136
New Zealand's figure matches its 2025 holiday-arrivals growth rather than the 5.9% for total
arrivals in 2025.137
Melbourne's 4.3% is an Australia-wide forecast rather than an actual,138
so the
comparator range runs from about 4% to 40%.
The Western Cape's two paths straddle the band. The base case implies 3.8% a year from 2025 to 2035
and the accelerated case 5.3%, and the accelerated case reverts to base "without confirmed routes".139
The Benchmarking Report counts growth as structural only above that band. It must also be "driven by
deliberate changes to demand drivers, access conditions or product systems, rather than cyclical recovery
or short-term promotion".140

Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
Cape Town is the axle of the system: international visitors arrive there and disperse from there, and "Cape
Town remains the engine of the system, but growth cannot keep concentrating there".141
The Department
of Economic Development already reads the growth framing as overweight on volumes and asks for a
value and spend lens in place of arrivals.142
Growth now needs new markets, new seasons and new
regions, and each of those is a supply problem before it is a marketing problem.
1.4 Approach and method
The study ran in four phases from November 2025, tested one hypothesis tree against every
interim deliverable and took each figure from a ranked hierarchy of sources.
Four phases
The Inception Report set out four phases with printed durations: inception, one month; demand-side
analysis, four months; supply-side analysis, three months; enablement and final reporting, seven
months.143
The Phase 4 detail slide prints six months for the same phase;144
the report uses seven, which
matches the Phase 4 bar on the deliverables gantt, May to the end of November.145
The deliverables gantt
runs November to November without printing years.146
Each phase had a distinct job.147
Inception aligned expectations and ways of working and produced the
Current State Diagnostic as a literature-based baseline.148
The demand phase covered source markets,
India and China route to market, benchmarks and trends; the supply phase covered spatial readiness and
community inclusion, and the two overlapped from February to April.149
The final phase built the M&E framework, consolidated the strategy into this report and prepared the
launch.150
Three mechanisms governed the work: fortnightly working sessions with Wesgro, Steering
Committee sessions at milestones and fortnightly written status updates.151
The session calendar ran from
the kick-off on 13 November 2025 to a final-report Steerco pencilled for 12 June 2026.152
The hypothesis tree
The analytical core is a hypothesis tree agreed in December 2025 and tested by every workstream since.
The Current State Diagnostic printed it with three pillars, six themes and eighteen entries.153
The pillars are
Reshape Demand, Transform Supply and Build Enabling Capability, each carrying two themes.
The tree disciplined the evidence. The Stakeholder Insights Report mapped all 223 of its insight
statements to tree entries before aggregating them.154
Because the study team mapped the insights to tree
entries, the counts show where views cluster; they do not on their own validate an entry. The later
deliverables reprinted the tree as a recap and marked the branch they addressed.
Three entries changed wording between December 2025 and March 2026.155
The seasonality entry gained
mega events; the institutional entry widened from Wesgro, DEDAT and CTT to local, provincial and

Source hierarchy
Every figure in this report carries a source and page, and sources rank in five tiers. Where tiers disagree
the higher tier wins; where two documents in one tier disagree, both values are recorded and the value
used is stated with the reason. Nothing is reconciled by averaging.
1. Interim deliverables, cited by the date printed on the cover and by PDF page index, since none
prints page numbers.
2. Working analyses: the Market Assessment Report of May 2026 and the three Skift memos on digital
and search insights (December 2025), segmentation (March 2026) and African markets (March
2026), cited by section.
3. Wesgro and government data cited through a deliverable:159
the Mero 2024 reports for all six
districts, the Pero 2025 and the Growth for Jobs Strategy 2023. The same tier holds the Tourism
Blueprint, Wesgro's five-year strategy and the StratEcon cruise liner economic contribution studies
for 2023, 2024 and 2024/25.
4. Benchmark and reference reports, such as the UN Tourism barometer behind the global growth
norm.160
5. External public sources, used only where no higher tier carries the figure and labelled as such.
Two conventions follow. The MIDAS Aviation Market Assessment and the Skift memos are Word files
without page numbers, so they are cited by section title. The 14 October 2026 summary deck ranks below
the deliverables: where the two differ, the deliverable's figure is used and the difference is logged.
Interim deliverables
Nine dated documents carry the evidence, and the table below dates each by its cover. The Market
Assessment Report of May 2026 is the working analysis behind the Demand-Side Analysis and alone
prints the 2025 base the growth case uses.161

Stakeholder engagement
Stakeholder engagement ran in four phases alongside the analysis.162
Introduction and framing ran from
January to March 2026, sense-making and validation in April and May, strategy socialisation in June and
July and mobilisation from August into October. The Stakeholder Insights Report closed the first phase
with insight tables for sixteen organisations, from Mossel Bay to the CTICC.163
Sixteen is a count of those
tables; the report prints no tally.
The method was strict about what an insight is. Sessions were run, insights extracted, each mapped to a
tree entry and then aggregated.164
The 223 statements are the project team's paraphrases rated High,
Medium or Low, not quotations, so this report attributes them to organisations and never to named
individuals.165
Two gaps in the record matter: phase 1 engaged no airline, online travel agent or tour operator, and the
six demand-side engagements were proposed for phase 2.166
[Open item: test the gateway and
governance chapters with ACSA, the Cape Town Air Access airline partners, the City of Cape Town and
Cape Town Tourism before Steerco.] The demand work drew instead on discussions with more than 20
stakeholders of its own.167
The outcomes of engagement phases 2 to 4 sit in no document in hand
[Letsema to supply the phase 2 to 4 engagement record for Appendix A6].
Data limits
Five limits in the data shape how the findings read. The headline arrivals series counts international
passenger arrivals at Cape Town International, not tourists, and Cape Town air arrivals stand proxy for the
province in the market shares.168
No approved methodology exists for provincial tourism GDP and jobs,
and no deliverable prints a Western Cape tourism employment baseline.169
The supply dataset behind the Infrastructure Report (Supply_Infrastructure.xlsx) holds no geography
below district, and its 17,195 records added after May 2026 are generated placeholders, as the study
team found when it checked the file. [Open item: confirm whether the Infrastructure Report's count of
50,332 assets includes those records; if it does, restate Tables 1.4, 2.11 and A.20 and the district shares
drawn from them.] The inclusion analysis runs on an OpenStreetMap asset layer, extracted on 16
September 2026, joined to Census 2011 sub-places, so its resident shares are fifteen years old.170
The growth model exists in two editions. The May Market Assessment runs from a 2025 base of
1,498,829 arrivals and the June Demand-Side Analysis from a 2024 base of 1,445,031.171
Both reach
2,175,778 in the base case and 2,516,282 in the accelerated case by 2035. The report runs the growth
case from the 2025 base, keeps FY24/25's 1.44m as the recovery baseline and states which is which
wherever both appear.

2.1 Demand-side analysis and market strategy
Concentration, seasonality and peak seats cap growth before demand does, and the base
case crosses the model's 1.8m seat line by 2030.
What was asked
The demand workstream was asked where Western Cape demand comes from, where growth will come
from and how Wesgro should pursue it. The inception report framed the phase as a deep dive into priority
markets to uncover growth levers from real-world demand and travel barriers.179
It had two parts.180
Market assessments were to prioritise source markets by volume, spend and air
access and profile the traveller types within them. Route-to-market analysis for India and China was to test
visas, air connectivity and the visibility of Western Cape product on global channels.
This work answers SLA Annexure B clauses 1.1.1, 1.1.7 and 3.2.3. [Clause wording to be checked
against the signed Annexure B.]
What was done
Two decks carry the work. The Market Assessment Report of May 2026 set the baseline, the growth
model, route to market and the traveller segments.181
The Demand-Side Analysis of June 2026 carried the
model forward and added the African opportunity and the business-events landscape.182
Three methods supply the evidence.183
Skift's 2025 values-based survey covered more than 25,000
travellers in 34 outbound markets, 17 of them Wesgro priority markets, with 800 to 1,600 respondents a
market.184
Desk review drew on Wesgro reports, Statistics South Africa and Google search data, and more
than 20 stakeholder discussions added market-facing judgement.185
The growth model builds a rate for each source market from four levers.186
They are outbound growth in
the market, the Western Cape's penetration of it, the airlift ceiling and structural conditions such as
currency and visas. It anchors on OAG aviation data for access and Skift traveller data for demand.187
Its
three scenarios vary the intervention, not the demand: "the difference between scenarios is not demand
potential, but the extent to which structural barriers are removed".188
The two decks print the same 2035 points from different bases. The May deck runs from a 2025 base of
1,498,829 and the June deck from a 2024 base of 1,445,031.189
This report uses the 2025 base.
The Current State Diagnostic of December 2025 supplied the baseline folded in here: source-market
concentration, the seasonal profile and the first segment work.190
Its figures are cited where they add to
the later decks.

Findings that survived into the tree
1. Five markets carry the province. The UK, Germany, the USA, the Netherlands and France supplied
63% of overseas arrivals in 2025, and the first three alone 52%.191
The same slide prints 61% in its text;
the report uses 63%, the sum of the printed shares. The December baseline showed the same pattern,
with the top five at 61% of Cape Town overseas air arrivals from January to August 2024.192
Those markets also pay the most. Average spend per visitor in 2024 ran from R32,900 for the USA and
R26,500 for the UK down to R8,600 for Namibia and R8,500 for Zimbabwe.193
The deck's own reading is
that high-spend visitors come from Europe and North America, "tying future value growth to slower-
growing source markets".194
2. Six months carry nearly two thirds of the year. December, January and November were the peak
months of 2025 at 12.7%, 11.5% and 11.3% of overseas visitors, while June fell to 6.1%.195
October to
March hold about 62% on that table once its twelve shares, which sum to 104.9%, are normalised. The
2024 Wesgro Visitor Tracking Survey gives 61% and 39%.196
The deck draws the conclusion that matters for strategy. Growth depends on "improving conversion in
Apr-Sep rather than pushing incremental demand into already busy peak months".197
3. Demand supports 2.18m to 2.52m arrivals by 2035. From the 2025 base, the model projects
1,822,634 arrivals in the conservative case, 2,175,778 in the base case and 2,516,282 in the accelerated
case, implied growth of 2.0%, 3.8% and 5.3% a year.198
The step from base to accelerated rests on
confirmed direct routes to India and China, above-baseline activation and more hub capacity into Cape
Town.199
The model states the dependency plainly: "without confirmed routes, volume reverts to base".200

Brazil); Strategic (Japan, South Korea).
Spend follows the mix. At fixed average spend per group, from R24,500 a visitor in mature markets to
R8,500 in regional ones, base-case foreign spend rises from a modelled R27.12bn in 2024 to R40.59bn in
2035.203
4. Seats bind at the peak before demand does. The model draws current air access as "a hard ceiling
at 1.8 million inbound seats", and the base case first passes it at the 2030 year point with 1,802,929
arrivals.204
The line is the model's assumption, not a measured cap (section 1.3). Only six year points are
printed, so the crossing falls between 2028, at 1,673,855, and 2030. The deck judges 2.1 million by 2035
achievable only with deliberate intervention to add capacity.205
5. Four segments fit the province, and each has a home market. Skift's survey identifies four values-
based segments most aligned with the Western Cape, together about 97 million addressable travellers.206
The deck marks the pairs to pursue: Curious Explorers in the UK and the USA, Creative Connectors in
India, Discerning Connoisseurs in China and the UAE and Reflective Adventurers in Germany.207
Curious
Explorers and Discerning Connoisseurs are "most aligned to the Western Cape's offering".208

The segments matter for spread as much as for volume. Curious Explorers offer the "strongest opportunity
for geographic dispersal beyond Cape Town", and Reflective Adventurers have "strong potential to
support seasonality mitigation" through year-round outdoor and wellness travel.209
6. India and China already arrive, through other carriers' hubs. An estimated 71,000 travellers fly from
India to Cape Town each year through connecting hubs and about 30,000 from China, together about
101,000 journeys.210
Addis Ababa and Dubai handle more than 80% of the Indian connections, and direct
Indian service is "unlikely before 2030 due to aircraft constraints at Air India and IndiGo".211
The barriers are named market by market.212
Chinese and Gulf travellers face slow, document-heavy visa
processes; India has no direct flight to Mumbai or Delhi and thin Indian-friendly adaptation; the Gulf lacks
Arabic-language booking and local-currency pricing.
Each market books through its own platforms. Chinese travellers discover on Douyin and Xiaohongshu
and book on Trip.com, Qunar and Fliggy; Indian travellers book through MakeMyTrip, Booking.com and
Expedia; much Gulf travel still runs through private advisors and concierge agencies.213
The deck's
prescription is to activate inside those platforms and shift investment "toward trade-led conversion rather
than broad awareness campaigns".214
7. Africa is a base and a growth market at once. The Western Cape recorded 323,971 tourist arrivals
from 16 reported African markets in 2024, 62% of them from Zimbabwe and Namibia.215
African visitors
spent R4.2bn in the province in 2025, with Nigerians and Ghanaians spending most per visit at R31,000
and R27,700.216
Skift models Nigeria's addressable market at 310,000 to 510,000 adult trips a year and
Kenya's at 240,000 to 400,000.217
The sources rank Ghana differently. The Demand-Side Analysis names Nigeria, Kenya and Ghana as
priority markets,218
while Skift's memo places Ghana among its fourth-tier watch markets for brand seeding
rather than conversion.219
Regional markets also steady the year: Namibia and Zimbabwe "add stability,
repeat demand and counter-seasonal volume".220
8. Business events are the strongest green-season lever. International association meetings in Cape
Town rose from 33 in 2022 to 58 in 2024, and 109 of the 136 meetings held over the three years fell
between May and November.221
Cape Town ranked 35th in the 2024 ICCA ranking and hosted 37 of South
Africa's 64 UIA-recorded meetings in 2025.222
Cape Town hotel occupancy was 53% in June 2023, the
gap such events fill.223

What this means for the tree
Validates FA01, Respond to growth segment traveller preferences. Four segments, each with
named home markets (finding 5).
Supports FA02, Create green-season demand with MICE and events. Business events cluster
from May to November, when leisure demand is weakest (findings 2 and 8). The seat side of the case
sits in 2.2.
Supports FA03, Make the whole province findable and bookable. Conversion runs through
market-specific platforms that Western Cape product must reach (finding 6).
Rewords FA04, Convert India and China hub traffic. About 101,000 hub journeys a year and no
direct Indian route before 2030 (finding 6).
Validates FA05, Increase yield in mature markets. The highest-spending visitors come from the
slowest-growing markets (findings 1 and 3).
Validates FA06, Build a regional foundation (Africa). A large regional base and two high-yield
growth markets (finding 7).
Supplies the binding constraint for FA07, Win direct routes and add seats on full routes, and
the visa barriers for FA19, Lead removal of infrastructure, visa and safety deficits (findings 4 and
6).

2.2 Aviation market assessment
Airlines run Cape Town's full routes for yield rather than volume and withdraw seats in
winter, so new seats must be won route by route.
What was asked
The aviation work answered the inception brief to review current airlift and planned route development as
part of route to market.226
MIDAS Aviation set itself four questions.227
How well has the Western Cape
developed new air services, and how does that record compare with similar markets? What challenges
and opportunities do the next five years hold, and which emergent markets matter?
This work answers SLA Annexure B clause 1.1.1.2. [Clause wording to be checked against the signed
Annexure B.]
What was done
MIDAS measured scheduled seats and estimated bookings for every Cape Town route. Bookings come
from the airlines' global distribution systems, weighted to the full market, and so "will not always reconcile
exactly with airport published data".228
The latest annual booking set runs to November 2025.229
Cape Town was compared with eight secondary airports chosen for peripheral geography and inbound
tourism: Calgary, Christchurch, Edinburgh, Keflavik, Larnaca, Marrakech, Santiago and Valencia.230
The
report is a Word file without page numbers, so it is cited here by section.
The Infrastructure Report of July 2026 assessed the physical side of access: Cape Town International,
George, the proposed Cape Winelands airport and the secondary airfields.231
Its airport findings are
summarised here because they set the ceiling the airlines fly into; its spatial findings sit in 2.5.
Findings that survived into the tree
1. Cape Town has already won its routes. Twenty-nine international routes serve the airport in 2026,
and Cape Town added 17 mainline international services between 2015 and 2025, more than Santiago
(14), Valencia (12) or Calgary (8).232
Other study documents count new routes from 2014 on a different
basis. Cape Town's share of South Africa's international capacity rose from 10% in 2010 to 25%.233
MIDAS calls the result "a 'what next' moment".234
Tactical gains remain in frequency and aircraft size. The
next phase needs a strategy that "looks for long-term gains rather than short-term wins".235
The new carriers lean towards Africa. Seven of the twelve carriers that launched Cape Town services
since 2014 fly to African points, and the largest by seats are Airlink and Ethiopian Airlines.236
Table 2.3. Twelve carriers have launched Cape Town services since 2014, seven of them to African points.

Airlink Various regional points 441,602
Ethiopian Airlines Addis Ababa 437,430

2. The network runs through hubs, and none of it reaches Asia. The seven largest international
markets are all hubs, with 2.7 million seats in 2026 against 2.0 million in 2019.237
Western Europe holds
38% of international capacity, the Middle East 23%, Southern Africa 17%, Eastern Africa 14% and North
America 6%.238
Most passengers on those hub routes are connecting. Emirates' connecting share is "above 80%
annually" and Qatar Airways' nine in ten.239
MIDAS records "the absence of any direct capacity to any of
the Asian markets", and the Diagnostic counts one airline serving Cape Town from Asia against ten from
Africa.240
3. The routes that exist are full. Eleven of the 27 international routes in the load table ran above 80% in
the year to November 2025, led by Newark at 88.6%, Atlanta at 84.3% and Frankfurt and London
Heathrow at 83.0%.241
Sixteen ran at 75% or more (computed from the same table).
The printed network total is 76.0% on 3,816,797 seats, though the 27 listed rows sum to 75.0%.242
Cape
Town's 79% average load factor is the joint highest of South Africa's five largest airports, with Lanseria,
against about 70% nationally, 75% for Africa and 84% globally.243

4. Airlines raise fares before they add seats. MIDAS expects that "rather than increase capacity
carriers will seek to increase revenues via higher selling fares rather than add new services", which "will
frustrate that desire for increased visitors".244
The report carries no fare data, so the claim rests on the
author's judgement of airline behaviour.
5. Winter seats are withdrawn. June carried 6.4% of Cape Town's 2025 international seats and
December 10.8%, with May, July, August and September at 6.9% each.245
Connecting traffic is "especially
critical in the low season",246
which ties the winter schedule to the hub carriers' own priorities.

6. Regional routes run full and can take more capacity. Luanda ran at 86.2% and 83.4% on its two
airports, Maputo 83.3%, Gaborone 82.3%, Mauritius 81.8% and Harare 81.3%, and MIDAS names all five
markets for further capacity.247
Domestically, Cape Town to Durban runs at 90.0% and warrants more
seats; other domestic routes sit between 70% and 80%.248
7. India and China are long-term, government-led prizes. About 71,000 Indian travellers reached Cape
Town indirectly in 2025, half from Mumbai, with Addis Ababa and Dubai carrying 81% of them; direct
service is "unlikely before 2030".249
China sent about 29,600, and its routes are "frequently an extension of
government-to-government relations", with Guangzhou and China Southern the likeliest start.250
MIDAS recommends early relationships between Wesgro, the Indian carriers and Indian government
authorities, and treats China as a long-term investment in awareness and indirect volume before any non-
stop service.251
Australia (61,000 indirect travellers) and Saudi Arabia (137,000 passengers, with Riyadh
Air's planned hub) are the next markets on its list.252
8. The airport, not the airlines, sets the outer limit. Cape Town International's single runway has no
parallel taxiway and caps movements at about 30 an hour, and two of the airport's seven parts are already
full.253
The R6.39bn runway relocation lifts capacity from 30 to 45 movements an hour, a 50% rise, and
completes around 2033 on the Infrastructure Report's timing, with terminal relief from about 2032.254
ACSA's latest schedule shows the runway finished by December 2029.255
The alternatives arrive sooner but carry less. George handled 911,000 passengers in 2025, above its
design limit, and a R310m terminal expansion doubles it to about 2 million by 2028 or 2029.256

2.3 Global benchmarking
The comparator destinations grew faster than the global norm by running institutions,
marketing and air access as one system aimed at a single binding constraint.
257
Infrastructure Report, Jul 2026, p106.
258
Infrastructure Report, Jul 2026, p109.
Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory 56
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
What was asked
The inception report asked for four to six global comparators aligned to the Western Cape's market
profile.259
It wanted the strategies and policies behind their growth and the lessons on air access,
investment, partnerships and visitor experience that transfer. This work answers SLA Annexure B clauses
1.1.5, 1.1.11 and 3.2.7. [Clause wording to be checked against the signed Annexure B.]
What was done
The Global Benchmarking Report screened 30 destinations against 12 criteria.260
The criteria fall in four
groups: market and access; destination and product structure; growth and inclusion; performance. Each
selected case was then read through one framework covering its demand profile, two proven growth
levers and their transferability to the Western Cape.261
The final comparator set is the report's six: Chile; Melbourne and Victoria; Valencia; Singapore; Kenya;
and Auckland and New Zealand.262
This report uses that set. Where a sharper example exists elsewhere,
it is cited as a public source and labelled outside the set.
The set moved twice before it settled. The inception report selected Chile, Porto, Vancouver, Melbourne,
Valencia and Singapore, and its screen placed Auckland seventh and Kenya eleventh, both marked not
selected.263
The Diagnostic then recorded that adding Namibia and Kenya "will support a more balanced
and regionally grounded benchmark set".264
The March report kept Kenya, added New Zealand and dropped Porto, Vancouver and Namibia. No
document in hand records why, and the change is logged. MIDAS ran its own aviation benchmark against
eight secondary airports, summarised in 2.2.
The report sets a global norm against which growth counts as structural. Global arrivals grow 3% to 5% a
year under stable conditions, more than 5% marks structural acceleration and 10% or more a structural
shift.265
UN Tourism's own figures put global growth at 11% in 2024, while recovery was still running, and
about 4% in 2025.266
Growth counts as structural only when it is "driven by deliberate changes to demand
drivers, access conditions or product systems, rather than cyclical recovery or short-term promotion".267
Findings that survived into the tree
1. Two of the six comparators grew well above the world. Chile grew 40.4% between 2023 and 2024
and Singapore 21%, against global growth of 11%; Kenya (14.6%), Valencia (13% to 14%) and New
Zealand (about 12%) grew slightly faster than the world.268
Melbourne's 4.3% is an Australia-wide forecast
of average annual growth rather than an actual,269
and New Zealand's 12% matches its 2025 holiday-
arrivals growth; total arrivals grew 5.9% in 2025.270

(Singapore); Kenya Tourism data as reported, May 2025 (Kenya); UN Tourism, World Tourism Barometer, Jan 2025 (world, 2024). Where the
report prints a range, the bar shows its midpoint: Valencia 13% to 14%.
2. Access comes first, and the state treats it as infrastructure. Santiago handles about 90% to 95% of
Chile's 5.2 million international arrivals, and its airport traffic rose from about 16 million passengers in
Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory 58
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
2010 to more than 26 million in 2019.271
A private concession won in 2015 funded about US$900 million of
expansion, under one of the world's most liberal aviation systems built on open-skies agreements.272
The report draws the lesson for the Western Cape directly.273
It says to "treat air access as core economic
infrastructure, not a tourism add-on" and to establish "a permanent, well-funded Public–Private Air Access
Fund". It also says to "actively lobby for air service liberalisation and simplified visa regimes".274
3. Marketing money follows routes. New Zealand "shifted from awareness-driven marketing to route
accountable demand generation", tying budgets to specific long-haul corridors and designing campaigns
around airline breakeven and shoulder-season loads.275
The programme reports more than 16,000
incremental bookings and a return of about NZ$55 million in 2023/24, supported by government-backed
early-stage route incentives and data shared between tourism bodies and airlines.276
4. Hubs can be converted, not just flown through. Singapore turns transit into visits through its
stopover programme, with the tourism board, Changi Airport Group and Singapore Airlines working "under
a coordinated strategy".277
It received 16.5 million international visitors in 2024, 21% more than in 2023,
and tourism receipts reached a record of about S$29 billion.278
Kenya positioned Nairobi as East Africa's
aviation, diplomatic and business hub, and its main airport now carries 68% of arrivals.279
5. Events steady demand and pull air capacity. Victoria curates a portfolio of events that "generate
repeat, predictable demand spikes across the year", and Melbourne "uses its event calendar to de-risk
and stimulate aviation demand".280
Hotel stock grew 21%, about 5,000 rooms, behind it.281
The
transferable step is "a unified event acquisition and calendar strategy across Wesgro, Convention Bureau,
City and Province".282
6. Quality is governed, not encouraged. Valencia adopted a destination-wide quality system across its
tourism businesses and placed coordination in "a permanent public–private structure led by Visit
València".283
It received 3.5 million international visitors in 2024, 13.9% more than in 2023 and 24.4%
above 2019.284
The required change is a provincial quality charter, with certification "visible across booking
platforms".285
7. The leaders measure value, not arrivals. New Zealand manages regional capacity to protect pricing
and recommends that destinations "replace arrival growth with yield-based regional performance
metrics".286
Kenya earned about US$1,460 per visitor in 2024287
by building conservation and community
participation into one governed product system.288
8. The system is the lever. The report's synthesis is that "sustained tourism growth is not created by
fragmented interventions, but by a coordinated destination system in which governance, demand
generation and access reinforce one another".289
Chile's coordination works best "when anchored around
a single strategic constraint", which for Chile was long-haul access.290

What this means for the tree
Supplies precedent for FA07, Win direct routes and add seats on full routes, and FA15, Crowd
in capital with targeted investment strategies. Chile's airport concession and air access fund; New
Zealand's route-tied marketing (findings 2 and 3).
Supplies precedent for FA04, Convert India and China hub traffic. Singapore's stopover
conversion and Kenya's hub strategy (finding 4).
Supplies precedent for FA02, Create green-season demand with MICE and events. Melbourne's
events-led system and unified calendar (finding 5).
Supplies precedent for FA11, Align visitor experience in-destination, and FA03, Make the
whole province findable and bookable. Valencia's governed quality and certification visible on
booking platforms (finding 6).
Supplies precedent for FA05, FA12 and FA14. Yield-based metrics, capacity discipline and
conservation-led product (finding 7).
Supports FA16, FA17 and FA18. One coordinated system of governance, marketing and access,
with shared data between tourism bodies and airlines (findings 3 and 8).
2.4 Stakeholder insights
Collaboration and data sharing are the barriers stakeholders raise most often, and the
supply-side constraints they rate most severe.
What was asked
The inception report committed the study to interviews, focus groups and roundtables with key
stakeholders, with their insights carried into the final report.292
This work answers SLA Annexure B clauses
1.1.6 and 3.2.8. [Clause wording to be checked against the signed Annexure B.]
What was done
Engagement runs in four phases: introduction and framing from January to March 2026, sense-making
and validation, strategy socialisation and mobilisation into October.293
The March report closes the first
phase. It holds insight tables for 16 organisations, from Mossel Bay to the CTICC, carrying 223 insights
rated 115 High, 102 Medium and 6 Low.294
The method had four steps: run the sessions, extract insights, map each to the hypothesis tree and
aggregate.295
The insights are the project team's concise statements, not verbatim quotations. This report
therefore attributes them to organisations and quotes them as insight statements.
The record has two gaps. No airline, online travel agent or tour operator sits in the first phase; six such
engagements, among them Trip.com, Emirates and SATSA, were proposed for the second.296
The
outcomes of phases two to four are in no document in hand. [Letsema to supply the phase 2 to 4
engagement record for Appendix A6.]

Findings that survived into the tree
1. Collaboration and data are the most widely shared concerns. Fifteen of the 16 organisations raised
industry and government collaboration and fifteen raised policy and data sharing.297
Data sharing also
carries the most insights (30) and the most High ratings (16).
Figure 2.6. Themes raised by stakeholder organisations
Source: Stakeholder Insights Report, Mar 2026, p20 to p46: number of the 16 organisations with at least one insight under each hypothesis-
tree element, counted from the appendix tables. The report prints no tally.
The organisations describe the data architecture as "too fragmented, lagged and proxy-based to provide a
reliable view of visitor volumes, spend, spread or economic impact at provincial level".298
Platforms for
collaboration already exist: FEDHASA points to JAMS, through which tourism associations coordinate with
Wesgro, the Western Cape Government and the City of Cape Town.299
2. The province cannot yet measure what the strategy targets. The national Department of Tourism
reports that "there is no approved official methodology for provincial tourism GDP and jobs estimates",
rated High.300
Stats SA cites 749 vacancies behind delays to the tourism satellite account and infers
provincial flows partly from ports-of-entry data.301
3. Supply constraints are rated the most severe. Of the 68 insights under Transform Supply, 44 are
rated High, against 23 of 64 under Reshape Demand.302
Weak public transport, congestion and unreliable
last-mile access are called "structural barriers to visitor movement within and beyond Cape Town".303
Peak
queues at the Table Mountain Aerial Cableway run up to three hours up and two hours down.304
4. Funding and institutions have weakened. The Department of Economic Development rates
destination funding "materially below peer destinations" and reports that tourism institutional capacity "has
eroded over the past decade at both provincial and municipal level", both High.305
National Treasury's

Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
MFMA Circular No. 131 of 31 March 2025 has disrupted municipal funding for local tourism bodies, most
of which "are not operationally sustainable without municipal funding support".306
Mandates blur at district level. The West Coast District Municipality reports "a mandate disconnect"
between district, local municipalities and local tourism organisations that leaves tourism development
without a clear owner.307
Fiscal pressure is turning attention to a tourism levy and blended finance.308
5. The binding levers sit nationally. The Department of Economic Development reports that "restrictive
bilateral air agreements are limiting airlift growth", rated High, and that SA Tourism's institutional
weakness constrains international marketing.309
Four organisations raised visas, among them the
Cableway for India and China and the CTICC for African events.310
Seven raised safety, which the report
calls a barrier across transport, township tourism and international selling.311
6. The flagship sites are at capacity and exposed to weather. Boulders is "already near carrying
capacity" and summit expansion on Table Mountain is severely limited.312
Robben Island loses about R16
million a year to weather-cancelled trips, and CapeNature reports climate-related flooding, fire and heat
closing sites.313
7. Much product cannot be booked online. CapeNature takes about 70% of its reservations through its
call centre rather than its online platform.314
The Department of Economic Development rates destination
platforms behind international practice in online presence and AI adoption, and the Overberg says its
proximity to Cape Town is not packaged before travellers book flights.315
8. Some constraints went unraised. No stakeholder named Cape Town International's capacity as a
constraint; the airport appears only as a transfer-time reference.316
Cruise is raised only by Mossel Bay,
where calls rose from four before COVID to 27 a season.317
The demand-side and airport voices were
scheduled for later phases, so their absence here is a gap in the record, not a finding.
The report closes on eight areas for deeper engagement, which frame the questions the later phases were
to test.318

What this means for the tree
Validates FA17, Strengthen industry and government collaboration, and FA18, Expedite
tourism-enabling policy and data sharing. Each raised by 15 of 16 organisations, with existing
platforms such as JAMS to build on (findings 1 and 2).
Rewords FA16, Align institutional stakeholders across all three tiers, and supports FA19, Lead
removal of infrastructure, visa and safety deficits. Air rights, visas and SA Tourism sit nationally;
mandates blur at district level (findings 4 and 5).
Rewords FA14, Manage climate risk and sustainable tourism, and supports FA15, Crowd in
capital. Unfunded local tourism bodies and climate losses at flagship sites (findings 4 and 6).
Supports FA03, FA09, FA10 and FA12. Product that cannot be booked online, last-mile access, a
narrow product set and flagships at capacity (findings 3, 6 and 7).
Supplies the case for FA20, Develop workforce and service skills, as a question. Skills gaps
raised by CapeNature, the West Coast and the Overberg, with no labour analysis behind them.319
2.5 Spatial and infrastructure readiness
Tourism readiness falls with every step away from Cape Town, so growth outside the Metro
depends on deliberate corridor investment rather than spillover.
The tourism economy and who shares in it
The supply-side evidence in this section and the next starts from the same fact: the tourism economy is
large and concentrated. Foreign visitors spent R25.9bn in the province in FY2025,320
and the Cape Metro
generates 72.5% of provincial GDPR.321
Little of that spend reaches the communities around the nodes. The 20 communities shortlisted by the
inclusion work hold 18.4% of the province's residents (Census 2011) but receive a traced floor of 0.02% to
0.04% of foreign direct spend, about R4m to R10m a year.322
Section 2.5 sets out where the supply sits;
section 2.6 sets out why so little of it is shared.
What was asked
The supply phase was asked to understand spatial infrastructure and readiness for scalable growth
through an asset inventory and readiness index, GIS mapping, regional profiles and an investment
opportunity framework.323
This work answers SLA Annexure B clauses 1.1.2, 1.1.8 and 3.2.4. [Clause
wording to be checked against the signed Annexure B.]
What was done
The Infrastructure Report mapped 50,332 tourism assets across the six regions in three layers:
attractions, services and enabling infrastructure.324
It drew on Wesgro's supply database, SANParks and
CapeNature data and open geographic sources.325
Its premise is that "destination performance is
constrained by the weakest layer".326

Each region is scored from 1 to 5 on attractiveness (40%), readiness and capacity (30%) and accessibility
(30%).327
The scores "are evidence informed, not purely formula derived", and missing data is not scored
as poor performance.328
The overall ratings are the Infrastructure Report's evidence-informed judgement,
not a weighted formula, so this report does not re-derive them.
Six touring routes were rated Ready, Developing or Gap on access, accommodation, safety and
bookability.329
The final report of July 2026 supersedes a June draft. [Dataset limits, including the absence
of geography below district level, to be confirmed against the supply dataset notes.]
Findings that survived into the tree
1. Readiness halves between the Metro and the periphery. The Cape Metro rates 4.5 out of 5, the
Cape Winelands and the Garden Route 3.4, the Overberg 2.7, the West Coast 2.5 and the Central Karoo
1.9.330
The report's summary is blunt: infrastructure is "eroding rapidly with distance from Cape Town,
weakening the visitor experience".331
Accessibility falls fastest.332
On attractions it scores 4.7 in the Metro, 3.3 in the Winelands and 3.0 on the
Garden Route. It drops to 2.0 in the Overberg, 1.7 on the West Coast and 1.0 in the Central Karoo; on
transport the Metro scores 5.0. "The further from Cape Town, the faster the infrastructure falls away".333

3. Beyond the Metro and the Garden Route the visitor drives. Public transport is "effectively absent"
on the West Coast and in the Overberg, the Winelands have no transit between estates or valleys, and
every accessibility measure in the Central Karoo "sits at the floor".337
Metro rail is "functionally unusable for
tourism".338
4. Strong assets sit in cold zones. The West Coast has the province's highest natural-asset
attractiveness and the Central Karoo its most unique niche assets, yet both score lowest on readiness.339
Inland areas north of the N2 on the Garden Route are tourism-inactive, and industrial belts in the Metro
show "zero tourism conversion" despite nearby attractions.340
5. The mid-market is missing. Mid-market accommodation "is effectively absent outside Stellenbosch" in
the Winelands, where a "luxury-skewed product mix excludes mid-market and family segments".341
Accommodation is thin beyond Langebaan and Paternoster on the West Coast and thin at international
grade along Route 62.342
The report gives no bed count for the gap.
6. Bookability fails first in the regions. About 40% or more of the Metro's smaller operators lack online
booking.343
Route 62 has the lowest online bookability of the six routes, and the West Coast route rates
Gap on digital distribution and payments.344

7. The flagships are full in season. Table Mountain Aerial Cableway queues run two to three hours at
peak, Chapman's Peak suffers disruptions and the December to January peak "overwhelms transport and
accommodation capacity".345
Cape Point and township attractions, by contrast, are underused.346
8. Committed capital closes the air gaps, not the regional ones. The Metro's pipeline exceeds R30bn,
led by ACSA's R11.3bn airport overhaul and the R20bn V&A Granger Bay expansion, and George's
R310m terminal doubles capacity by 2028 or 2029.347
Last-mile transit, small-operator bookability and
township product remain unfunded in the Metro, as do inter-node transport and winter product on the
Garden Route.348
Port spending is not going to cruise. Cape Town's single berth runs at about 40% of a season capacity of
150 or more calls, with 83 calls and R1.79bn of impact in 2024/25.349
Saldanha's R4bn upgrade is for iron
ore, and the report's advice is to grow cruise "through product and marketing, not capital works".350

What this means for the tree
Validates FA09, Accelerate tourism-enabling internal mobility. Accessibility decays from 4.7 to
1.0 and public transport is absent beyond the Metro and the Garden Route (findings 1 and 3).
Validates FA10, Support diversified route and product development, and FA11, Align visitor
experience in-destination. Strong assets in cold zones and a missing mid-market (findings 4 and 5).
Validates FA12, Balance impact on nature-based attractions. Flagships full at peak while
alternatives are underused (finding 7).
Supports FA03, Make the whole province findable and bookable. Bookability fails first in the
regions (finding 6).
Supports FA07, FA08 and FA15. Airport capital is committed; cruise needs product, not berths;
regional gaps have no committed capital (finding 8).
2.6 SME and community inclusion
Organisation, not distance, keeps tourism spend out of the province's townships and rural
settlements: 93 of 107 sit within 45 minutes of a tourism node.
Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory 69
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
Section 2.5 opened with the scale of the gap: the 20 shortlisted communities hold 18.4% of residents
(Census 2011) and receive a traced floor of 0.02% to 0.04% of foreign direct spend.351
This section
explains why.
What was asked
The inception report asked for a framework to bring SMMEs and communities into tourism, built from
value-chain analysis, a typology of barriers, evaluation of global models and an inclusion blueprint.352
This
work answers SLA Annexure B clauses 1.1.3, 1.1.9 and 3.2.5. [Clause wording to be checked against the
signed Annexure B.]
What was done
The SME and Community Inclusion Plan set out "to identify which township and rural communities are
missing out on tourism income, to understand why and identify practical routes to stimulate
development".353
It defines inclusion by where the activity sits: a tour, a meal, a bed or a market inside the
community counts; a resident commuting to a hotel job in town does not.354
The long-list takes every Census sub-place cluster of 1,500 people or more with official unemployment of
25% or more, plus named mission, fishing and farm settlements.355
That gives 107 communities holding
2.81 million people in 2011, 48% of the province.356
Five weighted criteria (need, tourism potential,
proximity, readiness and access) produced a shortlist of 20, five in the Cape Metro and three in each
district.357
The evidence joins Census 2011 sub-places to an OpenStreetMap asset layer extracted in September
2026, with road travel times and municipal plans.358
Need rests on Census 2011 because Census 2022 is
not published below ward level and asked no income question.359
Five benchmark cases, from Rio de
Janeiro to Kliptown, test what lasted.360
Findings that survived into the tree
1. The communities hold people, not spend. The 20 shortlisted communities hold 18.4% of Western
Cape residents and 15.4% of employed residents (Census 2011) but receive 0.02% to 0.04% of foreign
direct spend, about R4m to R10m a year on the documented floor.361
Capturing 1% of FY2025 foreign
direct spend would bring R259m a year.362
2. Distance is not the barrier. Of the 107 long-listed communities, 93 lie within 45 minutes of one of 35
tourism nodes or three corridors.363
Langa is 12 minutes from Cape Town International and Zwelihle seven
from Hermanus.364
Distance is the binding constraint in none of the 20.365
Proximity and access carried
35% of the shortlist score (Table A.21), so this result is partly built into the selection.
3. Organisation is. Organisation is the binding constraint in 11 of the 20, ten have no identified active
tourism body and nine show no benefit visible to residents.366
Product often exists without a seller: Langa's

The same pattern repeats. Zwelihle's four resident guides sell by phone with no shared booking channel,
and the Mitchells Plain festival drew 27,800 visitors in November 2025 with no local operator or LTO
