Source: SME and Community Inclusion Plan, Sep 2026, p30 (constraint tally across the 20 shortlisted communities).
4. Safety is the second gate. Nine of the 20 record a safety-perception gap and five record extortion.369
The plan treats safety as a launch requirement in its own right: "tourism activity alone cannot resolve crime
or extortion".370
5. The benchmarks point to one order. The cases converge on "a seller with a stake, a safe and
workable visit, product that keeps spend in the community, then exposure".371
In Rio, resident guides kept
selling after the programme that launched them ended; local selling mechanisms lasted where assets and
training without a route to market did not.372
Capital first fails. Kliptown received a first allocation of R436 million and was projected to draw almost
200,000 international visitors a year, but its museum drew about 4,000 visitors a year by 2017.373
Costa
Rica's community network turned over more than USD 800,000 in 2013 and dissolved after donor support
ended.374

6. Each archetype needs its own first move. Communities near a node with product already in place
need a seller and a slot in the node's itineraries.375
Those near a node with nothing inside need one
anchor asset, but only after a seller is in place. Remote communities need access first or, where nothing
sits inside, procurement and skills before any asset.376

7. The model is a seller, a link to demand and a set of gates. The plan's core model is a functioning
selling capability, a connection to demand the node already has and launch gates for safety, access and a
viable operating model.377
It proposes a pilot in two or three of six profiled communities, scaled only once
bookings, local spend, delivery and safety meet thresholds agreed before launch.378
The first deliverable is deliberately small: "one live booking route, published operating dates, a named
customer contact and a simple record of bookings and local revenue".379
Most of the illustrative first moves
carry no dedicated budget line.380
Roles and funding are set out in Chapter 4.
8. Nobody counts tourism jobs inside the communities. No profile counts tourism employment inside
a community boundary; the plan gives node-side figures instead, such as about 4,700 formal Town Centre
jobs for 82,393 employed Mitchells Plain residents.381
Need rests on fifteen-year-old Census data.382

What this means for the tree
Refines FA13, Embed local destination bodies for community inclusion. The binding constraint
is a seller with a stake, then a safe way in, then demand pulled from the adjacent node (findings 2, 3,
5 and 7).
Supports FA03, Make the whole province findable and bookable. Community product sells by
phone or through city operators (finding 3).
Supports FA10, Support diversified route and product development, and FA15, Crowd in
capital. Assets follow sellers and route products, not the reverse (findings 5 and 6).
Supports FA19, Lead removal of infrastructure, visa and safety deficits. Safety and extortion
gate activation in up to nine communities (finding 4).
Supports FA20, Develop workforce and service skills, and FA18, Expedite tourism-enabling
policy and data sharing. No count of tourism jobs inside the communities; need rests on Census
2011 (finding 8).

DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
Chapter 3
Findings and
recommendations
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
3.1 Pillar 1: Reshape demand
Latent interest is not what holds Pillar 1 back; turning it into bookings is. The province is
known in its mature markets, under-sold in its growth markets and hard to book outside its
core.
The December tree gave the pillar one objective: break through the market, seasonality and spread
ceilings by reshaping the brand toward new traveller segments.383
The evidence confirmed all three
ceilings.
• Markets. Five markets supplied 63% of overseas arrivals in 2025, and the UK, Germany and the
USA supplied 52% between them.384
• Seasons. June held 6.4% of the year's international seats at Cape Town International in 2025,
against 10.8% in December.385
• Spread. Most rural properties have no OTA presence and are invisible to the market.386
What changed between December and October is the diagnosis. The Market Assessment counts about
97 million high-value travellers in 34 markets with an interest in South Africa, of whom about 1.4 million
convert into arrivals.387
The Diagnostic had already named the constraint as conversion and access, not
only awareness.388
Three of the six entries were reworded toward conversion: green-season seats,
bookable product and hub traffic already in the air.
Pillar 1 carries most of the base case. Between 2025 and 2035 the base case adds 676,949 arrivals.389
The four hub markets supply 206,584, the mature markets 178,780, the acceleration markets 175,818,
regional Africa 110,298 and the strategic group 5,469. None of it lands without seats, so the air access
entry in Pillar 2 runs beside this pillar from the first phase.

3.1.1 Traveller, brand and proposition
The first theme is about whom the Western Cape sells to, when and how. It holds three focus areas: the
segments, the green season and the bookability of the province. The theme name carries through from
December unchanged.
T H E M E 1 . 1 · T R A V E L L E R , B R A N D A N D P R O P O S I T I O N · F O C U S A R E A 1
Respond to growth segment traveller preferences
Four traveller segments, each concentrated in named home markets, give Wesgro a sharper
target than country alone, and the province scores 3.7 to 4.6 out of 5 on its ability to win
them.

every reprint of the tree.

work.

Stakeholders also name segments outside the four: medical tourism, township culture, eco-adventure and

Recommendation
Wesgro should market by segment within market, not by market alone, and judge results by value as well
as volume. The UK and Germany anchor the near term with a combined pool of about 12.5 million, and
the USA is the largest single pool outside China.394
The Department of Economic Development asks for
the same shift, toward a value and spend lens over arrivals.395

Benchmark precedent
New Zealand gives each priority market a role, such as the USA as its high-yield growth engine, and
judges performance on revenue per visitor and length of stay over arrivals.396
The required change for the
Western Cape is to replace the arrivals KPI with yield-based metrics and build partnerships with specialist
distribution networks.397

T H E M E 1 . 1 · T R A V E L L E R , B R A N D A N D P R O P O S I T I O N · F O C U S A R E A 2
Create green-season demand with MICE and events
The green season is a seat problem and an events problem at once. June holds 6.4% of the
year's international seats, and business events are the lever best placed to fill the trough,
though international meetings now peak from September to November.
Hypothesis and disposition
December 2025. Develop the Seasonality and Spread Proposition,398
reprinted from March 2026 as
Develop seasonality, spread and Mega events proposition.399
October 2026: Reworded. June holds 6.4% of seats, and ICCA meetings cluster from May to November.
Spread moved to focus areas 3 and 10, and business events replaced mega events as the lever. MIDAS's
seasonality analysis and the Demand-Side MICE landscape drove the change.
Evidence
Seats and visitors both fall away from April to September. Business events do not.

3.1.2 Source market growth and diversification
The second theme is about which markets grow the base. It holds one focus area per market group: India
and China, which reach the province through hubs; the mature long-haul markets; and regional Africa. The
theme name carries through from December unchanged.
T H E M E 1 . 2 · S O U R C E M A R K E T G R O W T H A N D D I V E R S I F I C A T I O N · F O C U S A R E A 4
Convert India and China hub traffic
About 101,000 Indian and Chinese travellers a year already reach Cape Town, mainly
through Gulf and African hubs, and with no direct Indian route likely before 2030, converting
that flow is the lever available now.
Hypothesis and disposition
December 2025. Pivot to Growth Markets (China, India, ME, ASEAN).413
October 2026: Reworded, with the scope narrowed. About 101,000 travellers a year already fly in via
hubs. The Middle East and ASEAN left the title, and the lever narrowed from pivoting to converting traffic
already in the air. The Demand-Side route-to-market work and MIDAS drove the change. The Middle East
stays in the growth model's acceleration group.

3.2 Pillar 2: Transform supply
Supply sets the ceiling on the growth case. The airport caps arrivals before its relief arrives,
and outside the Metro the product exists but is hard to reach, book or stay in.
The December tree asked the pillar to remove mobility, infrastructure and product constraints so as to
expand the province's physical carrying capacity.431
The evidence confirmed the objective and located
each constraint.
• The gateway. The growth model draws current air access as a line at 1.8 million inbound seats,
and the base case crosses it at the 2030 year point;432
the runway binds at peak hours. The
international terminal upgrade at Cape Town International is due about 2032 and the runway about
2033.433
• The regions. Accessibility scores fall from 4.7 in the Cape Metro to 1.0 in the Central Karoo, and
one touring route of six is rated Ready.434
• The product. The constraint is less about creating new attractions and more about turning existing
assets into complete, accessible visitor experiences.435
Two structural changes happened in this pillar. The December entry on air access and cruise split in two,
because MIDAS gave air access its own evidence base. The Infrastructure Report found cruise to be a
product and marketing lever, with Cape Town's berth at about 40% of its season capacity.436
The entry on
infrastructure, visa and safety deficits moved to Pillar 3 because its levers sit nationally (section 3.3.2).
The Infrastructure Report reads transport as a connectivity and dispersal challenge, not a province-wide
capacity crisis, with Cape Town International the key gateway risk.437
Pillar 2 follows that reading: one
capacity entry at the gateway and five entries about reach, product and experience.
Table 3.8. Pillar 2 holds six focus areas: the air entry was reworded, cruise is new and the four others carried through
as Validated.

3.2.1 Accessibility, connectivity and infrastructure
The first supply theme covers how visitors reach the province and move through it. It holds three focus
areas: air access, cruise and internal mobility. The December entry on visa and safety deficits left this
theme for section 3.3.2.
T H E M E 2 . 1 · A C C E S S I B I L I T Y , C O N N E C T I V I T Y A N D I N F R A S T R U C T U R E · F O C U S A R E A 7
Win direct routes and add seats on full routes
Eleven of 27 international routes run above 80% load and carriers raise fares before seats,
so the province must win frequency and larger aircraft now, ahead of runway relief in about
2033.
Hypothesis and disposition
December 2025. Close the Air Access and Cruise Capacity Gaps.438
The air half of the entry became this
focus area and the cruise half became focus area 8.
October 2026: Reworded. The reason is full routes and fares before seats: eleven routes run above 80%
load and sixteen at 75% or more.439
MIDAS and the Infrastructure Report's air-access section drove the
change.
Evidence
Table 3.9. The network is full on its best routes, the airport is full at its runway at peak, and planned relief lands after
the base case crosses the model's seat line.
Finding Figure Source
Routes are full 11 of 27 tabled routes above 80% load and 16 at 75% or
more; network load 76.0%
MIDAS, Seasonality
Fares before seats Carriers on full routes will seek higher fares rather than add

Benchmark precedent
Chile treated its gateway airport as economic infrastructure: a private concession funded a ~US$900
million expansion at Santiago, and international seats grew ~40% to 50% between 2013 and 2019 under
open-skies agreements.443
For the Western Cape the lesson splits by tier. The province can scale its
existing route fund; open skies and private airport finance are national levers, so this report carries them in
the escalation agenda (focus area 19). Chile's 2024 surge also came mostly overland: land borders
carried 55.6% of arrivals and Argentina about 40%.444

3.2.2 Product and destination capacity
The second supply theme covers what visitors find once they arrive: product beyond the icons, the quality
of the stay and the limits of the flagship sites. All three December entries carried through as Validated.

3.3 Pillar 3: Build enabling capability
Pillar 3 decides whether the other two can be delivered and shared: who benefits, who pays,
who decides and who measures.
The December tree asked the pillar to build an inclusive, well-governed tourism system that supports
sustainable growth, returns and development.477
The evidence found each part of that system thin.
• Inclusion. Twenty township and rural communities hold 18.4% of the province's residents but
receive a traced 0.02% to 0.04% of foreign direct spend.478
• Investment. The capital pipeline funds airports, ports, roads and rail, and none of it reaches tourism
product, bookability or community assets.479
• Governance. Visas and air service agreements sit with national government, and mobility
mandates are split across three tiers.480
• Measurement. No approved methodology exists for provincial tourism GDP and jobs.481
This pillar changed most between December and October, and now holds eight focus areas. The inclusion
entry was refined and held at concept level. Sustainability was reworded to climate risk and local
destination funding, and institutional review to three-tier alignment. The deficits entry moved in from Pillar
2, and workforce skills was added and flagged as untested.
The evidence here is mostly qualitative. It rests on attributed stakeholder statements, the Diagnostic's
one-slide tier table, the Inclusion Plan and the Infrastructure Report's gap tables, so this pillar carries more
open items than the other two.

3.3.1 Investment, inclusion and sustainability
The first enabling theme covers who benefits from growth, how it is paid for and how it lasts. It holds three
focus areas: community inclusion, climate risk and investment. The inclusion entry stays at concept level,
as agreed with Wesgro on 24 September 2026; roles and funding sit in Chapter 4.
T H E M E 3 . 1 · I N V E S T M E N T , I N C L U S I O N A N D S U S T A I N A B I L I T Y · F O C U S A R E A 1 3
Embed local destination bodies for community inclusion
Twenty communities hold 18.4% of the province's residents but receive only a small
documented share of foreign direct spend, and in eleven the binding constraint is
organisation, not distance or product.

Recommendation
Wesgro should connect community product to the demand it already moves, and leave the building of

activation and investment.485

thresholds are met.486

through a safety plan and a fixed-date test, to investment after demand and scale through channels.487

3.3.2 Governance and leadership
The second enabling theme covers who decides and how the plan is run. It holds five focus areas, the
most of any theme. They are three-tier alignment, industry collaboration, data and policy, the national
deficits moved in from Pillar 2 and workforce skills, which was added in October and flagged as untested.

Expedite tourism-enabling policy and data sharing
No approved method exists for provincial tourism GDP and jobs, operators cannot see their
own customers and four arrivals measures run unreconciled, so a data-sharing protocol is
the precondition for every target.
Hypothesis and disposition
December 2025. Expedite Tourism-Enabling Policy and Data Sharing.514
October 2026: Validated. Already underway. The stakeholder evidence drove the disposition: data and
policy is the largest theme in the stakeholder record. The platforms are indeed underway, but no
deliverable describes a data-sharing protocol or a policy revision in progress at provincial level [data-
sharing protocol or policy revision in progress, with owner and start date: Wesgro Research and DEDAT
to confirm].

Lead removal of infrastructure, visa and safety deficits
Visas, air rights, the runway and policing sit with national government, so the province leads
by escalation backed with evidence, and delivers the safety programmes and basic services
that are its own.
Hypothesis and disposition
December 2025. Lead Removal of Infrastructure, Visa and Safety Deficits, under theme 2.1.521
October 2026: Moved. The entry moved from supply to governance because its levers sit nationally. The
stakeholder evidence, the Demand-Side barriers and MIDAS all place visas and air rights with national
government; the move itself was decided at the October review [Summary of Findings, 14 Oct 2026: the
deck's stated reason for the move to confirm]. The rationale holds for visas and air rights, while safety
programmes are a provincial function and basic services a local one.522

Develop workforce and service skills
The 300,000 tourism-linked jobs ambition has no baseline and no labour analysis behind it,
so the first step is to commission that analysis, and the skills plan follows from it.
Hypothesis and disposition
December 2025. No December entry; skills appear in no printed version of the tree.
October 2026: Flagged, untested. The jobs goal has no labour analysis. The entry was added in
October because the 300,000-jobs ambition has no labour analysis behind it, and skills constraints
surfaced only as side mentions.529
As agreed in the report structure, it is written as a recommendation to
commission the analysis, not as a finding.
Evidence

3.4 The integrated case
The three pillars make one case. Demand work fills seats only if the seats exist, supply work
pays only if the product can be booked, and both need the enabling base to last.
The growth model sets out three paths from the 2025 base of 1,498,829 arrivals: 1,822,634 in 2035 on the
conservative path, 2,175,778 on the base path and 2,516,282 on the accelerated path.534
The deck is
explicit that the paths differ by intervention, not by demand potential.535
Each step up the case therefore
rests on a set of moves, and the table below maps each set to the focus areas that deliver it.
Table 3.24. Each step of the growth case rests on a named set of interventions, and each set draws on focus areas in
all three pillars.
Path Arrivals in 2035 What the model assumes
Focus areas that
deliver it
Conservative 1,822,634 Existing air access held with no added frequencies or
routes; continued trade and marketing; growth in the
mature markets and current corridors
5 and 7 hold the base
Base 2,175,778
(+353,144)
Emirates, Qatar, Ethiopian and Turkish Airlines add
frequency and capacity; existing Wesgro programmes
expand; India, China and regional Africa grow through
hub connections
1 to 7, 9 and 10
Accelerated 2,516,282
(+340,504)
Direct India and China routes secured; dedicated
activation in India, China, the USA and the GCC; India,
China and the Middle East grow ahead of their source
markets
4, 7, 16 and 19, on top
of the base
Source: Demand-Side Analysis, Jun 2026, p19 (assumptions) and p20 (2035 end points); Market Assessment Report, May 2026, p19 (2025
base). Increments between paths are computed from the printed end points. The mapping to focus areas is this report's.
The gateway binds first, at peak. The growth model draws current air access as a line at 1.8 million
inbound seats, and the base case crosses it at the 2030 year point.536
Relief at Cape Town International
arrives about 2032 for the international terminal and about 2033 for the runway.537
ACSA now schedules
the realigned runway for December 2029, subject to contract award. This plan keeps about 2033 as its
planning date and treats the earlier date as upside.538
Between those dates, growth has to come from
larger aircraft and added frequency on the full routes, from hub-carrier capacity and, for domestic
connections, from George, whose terminal doubles by 2028 or 2029 (focus areas 5, 6 and 7).
The deck draws arrivals and seats against one axis and prints no seat table behind the 1.8 million line. The
crossing year is read from the chart: after the 2028 point (1,673,855) and by the 2030 point (1,802,929).
If the supply moves slip, the case stops at the seat line. The deck states that the projected growth
cannot be realised without additional capacity or frequency,539
and that without confirmed routes the
accelerated case reverts to base.540
On the full routes, demand work cannot make up the gap, because
marketing raises fares rather than seats;541
on the hub routes, which have seats to spare, it can.
Below the gateway, the pillars depend on each other. Green-season demand needs seats in the
months carriers now cut (focus areas 2 and 7). Dispersal needs product that can be booked and reached
(focus areas 3, 9 and 10). Inclusion needs a seller and a safe visit before exposure (focus areas 13 and
19). Every target needs data that today sits with SA Tourism, ACSA and the booking platforms (focus area
18).

4.1 From strategy to initiatives
Twenty focus areas become eighteen initiatives, one per focus area except where one
owner delivers two.
The plan follows one chain from vision to measure. Each level answers a different question. The pillar
says what must change and the focus area says where; the initiative says what Wesgro and its partners
do, and the indicator says how Steerco will know it has happened (section 5.1).
Table 4.1. Seven levels run from the vision to the measures, and each level has a fixed count in this plan.
Level Count What it answers Example
Vision 1 Where the province is going Grow international arrivals from 1.5m in
2025 toward 2.5m by 2035
Pillars 3 What must change Reshape demand
Themes 6 In which part of the system Source market growth and diversification
Focus areas 20 Where the evidence says to act Convert India and China hub traffic

Source: Chapter 3; traceability table, Appendix A1. Action count from the Chapter 3 action tables.
Two merges take twenty focus areas to eighteen initiatives. Segment-led marketing and mature-market
yield share one owner and one set of channels, so focus areas 1 and 5 form initiative I1. Institutional
alignment and industry collaboration share one forum, so focus areas 16 and 17 form I15. Every other
focus area has its own initiative, numbered I1 to I18 in one sequence so that the M&E toolkit can track
them on one list.
Leads are proposed, not agreed. They follow the Wesgro units named in the Inception Report's
stakeholder map and the actions in Chapter 3, and Steerco confirms them at its first meeting [Steerco to
confirm initiative leads and partners]. Six initiatives have a partner as the accountable lead, because the
main lever sits outside Wesgro's mandate: corridor mobility, quality standards, flagship sites, community
pilots, LTO funding and skills.

4.2 Sequencing and phases
Three phases run to 2035, and the first is weighted to the moves that change the 2030
constraint: seats, bookability, green-season events and the inclusion pilots.
The phases follow calendar years: Activate from 2027 to 2028, Expand from 2029 to 2031 and Sustain
from 2032 to 2035. Each phase ends at a decision gate tied to the Chapter 5 indicators (section 4.7). The
first gate, at the end of 2028, falls in Wesgro's FY2028/29, two years before the base case crosses the
model's seat line around 2030.542
Figure 4.1. Initiative timeline, 2027 to 2035
Source: Letsema, initiative portfolio (sections 4.1 and 4.3). Design means scoping, data, agreements and pilots; implement means delivery at
scale.
Phase 1: Activate, 2027 to 2028
Phase 1 works inside Wesgro's mandate and budget and on the levers that move before the airport can. It
holds and adds seats on the full routes and builds the air access fund (I6). It publishes the green-season
calendar (I2), counts and starts to close the bookability gap (I3) and starts two or three inclusion pilots
(I12). Steerco, the data-sharing protocol and the escalation agenda start in the first quarter (I15, I16, I17),
because every other initiative reports through them.
The marketing initiatives (I1, I4, I5) and cruise (I7) spend 2027 on design: segment targets, hub-carrier
agreements, the Nigeria and Kenya plans and excursion products. Seven initiatives use both years for
design (I8 to I11, I13, I14, I18). Their first task is a baseline or a case that no deliverable holds: corridor
542
Demand-Side Analysis, Jun 2026, p21.
Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory 125
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
mobility, product, quality, flagship capacity, climate risk and LTO funding, the investment pipeline and the
labour analysis. The gate at the end of 2028 asks three questions. Have seats on the full routes held?
Does the bookability baseline exist and is it moving? Has any pilot met its thresholds?
Phase 2: Expand, 2029 to 2031
Phase 2 scales what Phase 1 proved and meets the model's seat line. Hub conversion, regional Africa
and segment marketing run at scale. Corridor mobility and product move from design to delivery, on the
Garden Route, the Winelands and the Overberg first. The quality charter is adopted and phased by
district, and the investment pipeline reaches financial close on its first projects. The mid-term review in
2030 tests arrivals against the base path when the base case crosses the model's seat line, and checks
that the runway is on its published dates (section 5.6).
Phase 3: Sustain, 2032 to 2035
Phase 3 runs as relief arrives at Cape Town International: the international terminal about 2032 and the
runway about 2033.543
The direct India and China route cases built since 2027 go to carriers as capacity
frees up, which is the step the accelerated case depends on. Inclusion pilots that met their thresholds
scale through channels, climate risk and LTO funding are embedded in municipal planning, and the
terminal evaluation in 2035 closes the plan (section 5.6).

4.3 Initiative charters
Each initiative has a one-page charter that a lead can run from and Steerco can hold to
account.
Every charter uses the same fields. It states the objective, the focus areas served, the proposed lead and
partners and the tier of government. It then sets the actions in each phase, marked Design or Implement,
and closes with the dependencies, the funding route, the risks and the indicators it moves. Actions come
from the Chapter 3 action tables. Leads are proposals for Steerco to confirm, and rand bands are set in
section 4.6.

4.4 Roles and accountabilities
Wesgro is accountable for twelve initiatives and does part of the work in all eighteen; for the
other six, the accountable lead sits with another tier, and the plan names it.
The matrix below assigns each initiative to the four roles: responsible (R) for doing the work, accountable
(A) for the result, consulted (C) before decisions and informed (I) of progress. Where Wesgro is
accountable and does the work itself, the cell reads A/R. Assignments are proposals for Steerco to
confirm, built from the actions in Chapter 3.
Table 4.3. Wesgro is accountable for twelve initiatives; provincial departments, municipalities or site operators are
accountable for six, and SOEs or national departments do part of the work in four.

ACSA, Transnet and PRASA. National: Home Affairs, Transport, National Treasury and SAPS. Industry: industry bodies, airlines,
CapeNature, SANParks and attraction operators.
National government and the state-owned entities
Four levers sit outside the province and cannot be bought or marketed into place. Route rights and visas
sit with the national tier,544
the airport and port capital programmes with national SOEs545
and policing with
544
Current State Diagnostic, Dec 2025, p58.
545
Infrastructure Report, Jul 2026, p103, p112.
Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory 146
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
SAPS. The plan treats them as an escalation agenda (I17), carried by Steerco through DEDAT to the
national departments and SOEs. Each item is stated as a decision, an owner and a date the province is
asking for. Wesgro's own route work (I6) runs inside the existing agreements meanwhile.
Provincial government
DEDAT is a standing member of Steerco and the accountable lead where the lever is policy or funding
rather than promotion: quality standards (I10), LTO funding and climate risk (I13) and the labour analysis
(I18). The Department of Mobility leads the transport half of corridor mobility (I8). Wesgro's units lead the
demand, access, product and data initiatives and report through the programme office.
District and local government
Municipalities and LTOs hold the assets, the selling bodies and the visitor services that the regional and
inclusion initiatives depend on. They lead the community selling bodies in the pilots (I12), attach
covenants to public assets (I12, I14) and carry the route and product work in their districts (I3, I9). The City
of Cape Town and Cape Town Tourism are partners on events, mobility and data (I2, I8, I16) and sit on
Steerco.
The private sector and site operators
Airlines decide frequency and gauge, hotel investors decide the mid-scale tier and attraction operators
manage their own capacity. The plan gives them the evidence and the demand case. It asks for the
decisions it cannot make for them: seats on full routes (I6), investment in secondary nodes (I14) and
visitor-flow management at flagship sites, where the operators are accountable (I11).

4.6 Funding and investment
Most of the plan runs on budgets that already exist; the capital it needs sits with ACSA,
PRASA, Transnet and private investors, and none of it has been costed yet.
No deliverable costs any initiative. The Inception Report scoped an investment opportunity framework with
return scoring, but no separate output was delivered,546
and stakeholders describe Western Cape tourism
funding as materially below peer destinations without giving a figure.547
This section therefore sets the
funding route for each initiative and leaves the rand bands to be set with Wesgro Finance and DEDAT
[cost band per initiative and phase: Wesgro Finance and DEDAT to set before Steerco signs off the plan].

Crowding in capital. The rule for capital is the Inclusion Plan's: invest after demand is shown, and attach
a maintenance and marketing covenant to any public asset.548
Wesgro's investment promotion work builds
the tourism pipeline from the Infrastructure Report's gap-closure tables, where every district lists tourism
gaps with no committed capital (I14).549
The air access fund scales Cape Town Air Access's existing route
fund with public and private money (I6).
What the SLA asks and what is still to do. The SLA asks for return projections on infrastructure
priorities (clause 1.1.8.3) [clause wording to be checked against the signed Annexure B]. Those need
costed projects, and the only costed items in hand are the airport, rail and port programmes the province
does not fund. The return case is therefore an output of I14 in Phase 1, built project by project as the
pipeline forms.

4.7 Delivery governance
One Steerco, one programme office and four fixed decision points keep eighteen initiatives
and three tiers of government on one plan.
Figure 4.3. Delivery governance structure
Source: Letsema, proposed delivery governance.
Steerco. A Tourism Growth Steerco co-chaired by Wesgro's CEO and DEDAT's Head of Department,
with Cape Town Tourism as a standing member and the City of Cape Town, the district municipalities and
the industry bodies at the table. It is distinct from the Unlocking Tourism Steerco that oversees this study.
It receives the quarterly scorecard, clears cross-initiative issues, holds the escalation agenda and
recommends gate decisions to the Board. Stakeholders asked for this kind of alignment directly: mobility
responsibilities alone are split across three tiers of government.550
Programme office. A small office inside Wesgro runs the plan on a build, operate and transfer basis. It
builds the charters, the dashboard and the reporting calendar in Phase 1, runs them through Phase 2 and
hands them to the line units by the end of 2031. It owns the scorecard and the dashboard, and Wesgro
Research owns the indicator definitions and the data protocol (I16).
Cadence. Initiative leads report monthly to the programme office, Steerco meets quarterly and the Board
receives an annual outlook, which also anchors the Unlocking Tourism business outlook event. The plan
is refreshed each year against the indicators and the gate decisions.
Table 4.6. Four decision points test the plan against its indicators, and each one decides what the next phase funds.

4.8 Risks and dependencies
Ten of the fourteen material risks sit outside Wesgro's control, so the mitigation for most of
them is early warning and a route to the tier that can act.
Likelihood and impact are Letsema's assessment on a three-point scale, from the evidence cited in each
row. Steerco reviews the register each quarter and the owner reports on any risk rated high on both.

5.1 Results chain
Every initiative is measured at three levels: what it produces, what changes because of it
and what that adds to the 2035 case.
The framework is the written form of the M&E toolkit the SLA requires (clauses 1.1.4 and 1.1.10). It sets
indicators, baselines and targets for arrivals, spend, jobs, airlift, cruise and SMME inclusion, with the tools
to track them [clause wording to be checked against the signed Annexure B]. The Inception Report adds
geographic spread and asks for definitions, sources, frequency, owners and governance for each
indicator.551

Source: Letsema, results chain for the plan.

5.2 Indicator set
Seven impact indicators, twenty-five outcome indicators and thirty-six output indicators
measure the plan, each with a definition, a source, an owner and a frequency.
Tier 1 measures the 2035 case and is reported to the Board. Tier 2 measures the focus areas, one or two
each, and is reported to Steerco each quarter where the source allows. Tier 3 measures the initiatives'
own outputs and is reported by the leads to the programme office each month. The full register, with
targets, sits in the toolkit (section 5.7).

5.3 Baselines and targets
The arrivals targets come straight from the growth model, while the jobs and other Tier 1
targets wait for baselines that do not yet exist.
Baselines are fixed at 2025, the growth model's base year, for the growth-model indicators and at
FY2024/25 for the airport series, the recovery baseline used in Chapter 1. Targets follow two paths: the
base case, which assumes Wesgro's current activity continues with hub carriers adding capacity, and the
accelerated case, which adds direct India and China routes and dedicated activation.552
The model prints
the base case for 2028, 2030, 2032, 2034 and 2035, and the other paths for 2035 only. The 2031 base
value and the accelerated milestones are computed and marked as such.
Figure 5.2. Arrivals paths and milestones to 2035
Source: Market Assessment Report, May 2026, p19 (2025 base); Demand-Side Analysis, Jun 2026, p20, p21 (2028 and 2035 points; 1.8m
seat line). The 2031 base value is the geometric mean of the printed 2030 and 2032 points; the accelerated and conservative milestones are
computed at a constant rate between the printed 2025 and 2035 values.

Arrivals. The model's 2025 base and the airport series measure different things: arrivals on the growth
model's basis, which Skift is to confirm (section 1.1), and international passenger arrivals at Cape Town
International. The airport series is the quarterly proxy because ACSA publishes it, and it stood at 1.44m in
FY24/25 and 1.57m in FY25/26.553
Both are reported, each against its own history.
Spend. T1-03's path holds spend per arrival fixed, so its targets are in constant 2024 rand, while SA
Tourism reports nominal rand; progress is read after deflating by CPI. The model's 2024 spend of
R27.12bn sits 19% above the R22.7bn SA Tourism reported for FY2024.554
[Open item: Skift to rebase the
spend path on the FY2025 actual of R25.9bn.]

analysis sets a provincial measure (I18).

5.4 Data sources and gaps
Arrivals, seats and cruise volumes can be tracked from existing feeds; spread, bookability,
jobs and community spend cannot until new data is built or shared.
Stakeholders put data among the top barriers: no approved methodology exists for provincial tourism GDP
and jobs,559
and no one holds a single view of visitor behaviour.560
The data-sharing protocol under focus
area 18 (I16) is the route to closing the gaps below.

5.5 Tracking mechanism
A quarterly scorecard to Steerco, an annual outlook to the Board and a live dashboard
where the sources allow it.
Four principles run the tracking. Automate before collecting by hand: arrivals, seats and cruise volumes
come from feeds that already exist. Name one owner for the dashboard (the programme office) and one
for the definitions (Wesgro Research). Report every indicator against its own baseline and both paths.
Refresh the register each year at the plan review.

5.6 Evaluation and learning
Two evaluations bracket the plan: a mid-term review in 2030, when the base case crosses
the model's seat line, and a terminal evaluation in 2035.
The mid-term review tests whether the plan is on its path and whether the path still holds. It falls in 2030
because that is when the base case crosses the model's 1.8 million seat line,561
before runway relief on
the planning date.562
The terminal evaluation in 2035 measures the result against both paths and sets up
the next plan. Both should be run by a party independent of the programme office.
Table 5.11. Each pillar carries two evaluation questions, one on results and one on the assumptions behind them.
Pillar Did it work? Does the logic still hold?
1 Reshape
demand
Did arrivals, spend per visitor and the green-
season share move with the activation, and in
which markets and segments?
Is conversion still the constraint, or has access
overtaken it in the growth markets?
2 Transform
supply
Did seats, readiness and bookability rise outside
the Metro, and did visitors follow?
Did the airport relief arrive on time, and did the
second gateways carry the load meanwhile?
3 Build enabling
capability
Did the pilots meet their thresholds, and did
community spend rise?
Did the national asks get answers, and did the
data protocol deliver the baselines?
Source: Letsema, proposed evaluation questions.
Pilots before capital. The inclusion pilots are evaluated before any capital is committed, on the Inclusion
Plan's own measures: bookings, visitors and locally retained revenue.563
A pilot scales only when it meets
the thresholds agreed before launch.564
The same rule applies to any product or asset investment under I9
and I14: test demand, then invest.
Learning into the plan. Findings from each review, gate and pilot go into the annual plan refresh under
section 4.7, so the charters, targets and sequence change with the evidence rather than at the end of the
plan.

A2 Demand model
The demand model takes international arrivals from 1,498,829 in 2025 to 1,822,634,
2,175,778 or 2,516,282 in 2035, and its base case crosses the 1.8 million seat line the
model draws, at the 2030 year point.
Method
The model sets a growth rate for each source market from four levers and projects arrivals from the base
year to 2035.565
The levers are source market growth, Western Cape penetration of source-market
outbound, an airlift capacity ceiling and structural market conditions. Source-market growth sets the
ceiling: "WC cannot sustainably grow faster than it's source market is generating travellers".566
Projections
are anchored on OAG aviation data and Skift global traveller data.567
Scenario definitions
The three scenarios vary intervention, not demand: "The difference between scenarios is not demand
potential, but the extent to which structural barriers are removed and growth opportunities are actively
pursued".568
Table A.3. The scenarios step up from no new airlift or growth-market spend to direct India and China routes with
dedicated activation in four markets.

Seat line and model limits
Both decks state that "Current air access places a hard ceiling at 1.8 million inbound seats, meaning the
projected growth cannot be realised without additional capacity or frequency".576
Neither deck prints the
seats behind the line or a load factor that converts seats into arrivals. At the 79% average load factor, 1.8
million seats would carry about 1.42 million arrivals, below the 1.57 million recorded in FY25/26, so this
report reads the line as the model's assumption, not a measured cap (section 1.3).

A3 Benchmark cases
Six comparators were drawn from a 30-destination screen on twelve criteria, and each case
reduces to a system type, two levers and the changes they require of the Western Cape.
Evaluation criteria
Both the Inception Report and the Global Benchmarking Report screen destinations on twelve criteria in
four groups.577
Each destination received one average score across all criteria.578

A4 Readiness scores
The infrastructure assessment classifies 50,332 assets in six districts and scores each
district on attractiveness, readiness and accessibility; overall ratings fall from 4.5 in the Cape
Metro to 1.9 in the Central Karoo.
Method
The assessment models tourism supply in three layers (attractions, services and infrastructure) and holds
that "destination performance is constrained by the weakest layer".590
Its asset database sorts the layers
into twelve categories with named subtypes.591
It draws on Wesgro, SANParks, StatsSA and CapeNature
data,592
with Overpass, WikiQuery and tourism reports for 2023 to 2025 named as sources of geographical
data points.593
Table A.15. The asset database sorts tourism supply into three layers and twelve categories, from beaches and wine
estates to hospitals and police stations.
Layer Category Subtypes
Attraction Natural Beach; mountain; park & reserve; scenic routes
Attraction Cultural & heritage Museum; historical site; monument; cultural precinct
Attraction Commercial and leisure Wine estate; theme park
Attraction Events Festival; sporting event; cultural event; business events
Service Accommodation Hotel; guest house; lodge; self catering
Service Food & beverage Restaurant; café; bar
Service Tourism services Tour operator; travel agency; car rental
Service Visitor amenities Fuel station; retail centre; visitor information centres
Infrastructure Air access Airport; airstrip
Infrastructure Road network National route; regional road
Infrastructure Transport nodes Bus terminal; train station
Infrastructure Public services Hospital; police station
Source: Infrastructure Report, Jul 2026, p10, as printed in sentence case.
The comparative tables score a different set of eleven categories in three groups,594
and the district

accommodation, attractions, eateries, events and amenities.595

Source: Infrastructure Report, Jul 2026, p17, as printed. Scale labels: 1 'Very weak', 2 'Weak', 3 'Functional', 4 'Strong', 5 'Leading'. Stated
method: 'Average indicator scores within each dimension, then combine dimensions using the agreed weights.'
Each dimension is the average of its indicator scores, and the dimensions are then combined at
40/30/30.596
All three use a common scale from 1 to 5, with Functional at 3 and Leading at 5.597
The report
states four assumptions.598
Asset volume does not equal readiness, and one framework applies to all
regions. Indicator scores are evidence informed rather than purely formula derived, and missing data is
not treated as poor performance.
Overall rating. The overall ratings are the Infrastructure Report's evidence-informed judgement, not a weighted
formula, so this report does not re-derive them.

A5 Inclusion fact base
The Inclusion Plan scores 107 long-listed communities down to a shortlist of 20, rates their
readiness, sizes the spend they could capture and takes its order of action from five
benchmark cases.
Long-list rule and scoring weights
The long-list takes every Census 2011 sub-place cluster with 1,500 people or more and official
unemployment of 25% or more, plus named mission, fishing and farm settlements.607
The rule yields 107
township and rural communities with 2.81 million people in 2011 (48% of the province) and 3.79 million on
the 2025 estimate.608
Of the 107, 93 sit within 45 minutes of one of 35 supply-report nodes or three
corridors.609
By region the long-list holds Cape Metro 26, Cape Winelands 16, Overberg 19, Garden Route 23, West
Coast 13 and Central Karoo 10.610
The shortlist takes five from Cape Metro and three from each other
region.611
Need rests on Census 2011 because Census 2022 is not published below municipality and ward
and asked no income question.612

Archetypes and responses
Two tests set the archetype: a 45-minute drive time to the nearest node or corridor and whether any
attraction sits inside the boundary.613
Atlantis is held remote by Wesgro naming at 26 minutes from
Darling.614
For the largest group the plan's diagnosis is that "the product and the node's demand are there;
the seller is missing".615

Benchmark cases and the order of action
The five cases point to one order of action: "a seller with a stake, a safe and workable visit, product that
keeps spend in the community, then exposure".625
The plan's six takeaways are that a sustainable local
operator is essential, resident ownership matters, safety is a prerequisite, local spend should be captured,
existing product should be sold and spending should be tracked.626
Table A.25. The plan finds that local selling mechanisms lasted while assets and training without a durable route to
market did not, but Costa Rica's selling network did not survive donor exit.

A6 Stakeholder inputs
Phase 1 engaged 16 organisations, whose 223 rated insight statements span all 18
elements of the hypothesis tree and inform eight priority areas for deeper engagement.
Engagement record and method
Engagement runs in four phases: Intro & Framing from January to March 2026, sense-making and
validation from April to May, strategy socialisation from June to July and strategy mobilisation from August
into October.627
The March deck closes phase 1 and marks phase 2, a validation round with a select few
stakeholders, as still to commence.628
[Phase 2 to 4 records not in hand: Letsema to supply the
organisations engaged, session dates and outcomes for each phase.]
The project team held structured sessions with key stakeholders, extracted insights, mapped each to the
project hypothesis tree and aggregated them.629
Each insight is a concise statement written by the team,
not a verbatim quotation.630
Every row carries a pillar, a tree element and one impact rating of High,
Medium or Low.631
The deck does not define the ratings or record session dates, participants or roles, so
this appendix attributes insights to organisations and does not quote them.

A8 M&E toolkit
The toolkit register adds the source, frequency, owner and target to each Tier 2 indicator in
section 5.2.
Targets come from the growth model where it prints them and from the Inclusion Plan where it sets a pilot
count. Elsewhere the target is a direction or a milestone, marked proposed where it is this report's choice;
Steerco fixes the levels at Gate 1 once the 2027 baselines exist. Tier 1 targets are in section 5.3 and Tier
3 outputs in the charters.

Scorecard and dashboard templates
The quarterly scorecard carries one row per indicator: code, name, baseline and year, latest value and
date, direction against the path, target, source and owner. The annual Board outlook adds the Tier 1 chart
against both paths and the gate status. The dashboard data model lists each feed, its refresh frequency
and the manual-entry form for indicators without a feed. The templates themselves are delivered with the
Excel toolkit [M&E toolkit workbook: Letsema to deliver with the final report].

Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
A9 Glossary and references
Eleven terms carry a specific meaning in this report, and twenty-eight documents support it,
cited by the date on their covers.
Abbreviations are listed in the front matter. Deliverables are cited with the date printed on the cover; where
the file date differs, both are shown below. Working analyses rank below the deliverables, and public
sources are used only where no deliverable carries the figure.
Table A.32. Eleven terms have a fixed meaning in this report.
Term Meaning in this report
Accelerated case The growth model's upside path: direct India and China routes secured with dedicated activation;
2,516,282 arrivals in 2035
Base case The growth model's most likely path: Wesgro's current activity continues and hub carriers add
capacity; 2,175,778 arrivals in 2035
Cold zone The Infrastructure Report's term for an area with tourism assets but little or no tourism use
Disposition What the evidence did to a December hypothesis: Validated, Refined, Reworded, New, Moved,
Flagged or Withdrawn (section 1.5)
Focus area One of the twenty entries on the October 2026 hypothesis tree, each with a Chapter 3 entry, an
initiative and indicators
Green season April to September, the months of lower overseas demand
International arrivals On the growth model's basis, which Skift is to confirm; distinct from international passenger
arrivals at Cape Town International, which ACSA reports
Readiness The Infrastructure Report's score for how ready a district's assets are to receive visitors, beside
attractiveness and accessibility
Seat line The 1.8 million inbound seats the growth model draws as current air access, as drawn in the
Demand-Side Analysis; a model assumption, not a measured cap (section 1.3)
Seller with a stake The Inclusion Plan's term for a local body that sells community product and keeps the revenue in
the community
Tier 1, 2 and 3 Impact, outcome and output indicators (section 5.2)
Source: Letsema, from Chapters 1 to 5.
